# Amazon - Chinese-nexus operator's Cairn agents found and exploited a MONAI Label zero-day at East Asian universities

- ID: amazon-cairn-monai-label-zero-day-universities-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-20
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/amazon-cairn-monai-label-zero-day-universities-2026-11/

## Summary
CJ Moses (Amazon Threat Intelligence, AWS Security Blog, 20 November 2026) describes a Chinese-nexus operator using the open-source Cairn framework (https://github.com/oritera/Cairn). On 10 September 2026 Amazon found a publicly reachable Cairn instance with no authentication. Its operational history was visible, including project configurations, actor prompts, and AI output. Prompts were in Simplified Chinese, and timestamps in executed scripts used China Standard Time. The visible campaign lasted about 48 hours starting 8 September 2026 and targeted IP addresses of universities in East Asia outside mainland China. The operator gave target address lists and variations of the goal to obtain shell access. The agents then planned, executed, and changed approach without the operator choosing each next step. One operation spent about 16 hours and 148 sequential actions on an unauthenticated Redis instance at one university. Amazon says the agents tried known Redis control techniques, including replication abuse to load code, a Lua sandbox escape, configuration-file writes, and memory corruption, then a Log4Shell attempt against another service on the same host. After those failed, the agents used server-side request forgery in a co-located Qdrant instance to map the internal Docker network and sprayed credentials at PostgreSQL, SSH, and other services. That operation failed. A separate operation of more than 6 hours targeted a university GPU server. The agents built about 80 real usernames from faculty directories, lab sites, archived pages, and papers, then tried more than 30,000 credential combinations against SSH, an admin dashboard, a NAS, a VPN server, and the campus portal. That operation also failed. The successful operation targeted an open-source medical-imaging AI application. BleepingComputer and The Register identify that application as MONAI Label. The agents fingerprinted the framework, mapped API endpoints, found an upload endpoint with a filename parameter, and confirmed path traversal. Amazon says the gap from API mapping to arbitrary file write was about 11 minutes. A later session wrote a Python reverse shell into sitecustomize.py, which Python imports at startup, triggered a training API endpoint so a new Python process would load it, and obtained a root reverse shell. GHSA-qc22-rmgc-vg94 (published 18 November 2026, reported by aws-sec-vdp, fixed in pull request 1915) says PUT /datastore/image takes an unvalidated image parameter and PUT /datastore/label builds its path the same way. The advisory's example value is a relative path such as ../../../tmp/x. Authentication is off by default (MONAI_LABEL_AUTH_ENABLE=False). When authentication is on, the ANNOTATOR role is enough. Affected versions are 0.8.5 and earlier. The advisory's patched-versions field is None, and it says the fix will be in the next MONAI Label release. CVE-2026-133968 (GHSA-2f43-rwqm-6h67) describes the same traversal and says it was exploited in the wild starting in September 2026. Amazon assessed the bug as a zero-day with high confidence because, at the time of the activity, it had no matching fix, issue, advisory, or vulnerability-database entry. After the agents found the bug, the operator supplied a proxy that tunneled into a second university network. The server at the far end ran the same vulnerable software. Amazon infers the operator had already used the zero-day there to build the tunnel, and then directed the agents at other machines on that network. Attribution is Chinese-nexus at moderate confidence, from language, timezone, and targeting. The universities are not named. The victims were not AWS customers. Amazon notified the maintainers and worked with a government partner to notify the victims. Amazon also points to a separate Gambit Security report on a financially motivated actor using Cairn against online retailers. That report is not this campaign. Link unit42-cairn-cl-sta-1238-2026-11 and cairn-norforge-2026-10.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed Chinese-nexus operator. Amazon Threat Intelligence assesses moderate confidence from Simplified Chinese prompts, China Standard Time script timestamps, and targeting of universities it describes as likely of strategic interest to China. This is not CL-STA-1238.

- Actor: Chinese-nexus (unnamed)
- Country: China
- Motivation: unknown
- Attributed by: Amazon Threat Intelligence
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Operator-supplied university IP addresses and a goal to obtain shell access, run by Cairn agents. The successful path was an unauthenticated path traversal in MONAI Label's datastore upload API. The Redis and credential-spray operations failed. The initial access vector for the Cairn host itself is unknown.
- Victims and sectors: Universities in East Asia outside mainland China. One university Redis host and one GPU server were attacked without success. One MONAI Label server was compromised with a root shell. A second university network was reached through an operator-supplied proxy. Amazon does not name the universities.
- Regions: Asia
- Region notes: Amazon says the targets were universities in East Asia outside mainland China and does not name the countries. Mainland China is excluded by that wording, so it is not a victim region. Simplified Chinese and China Standard Time are operator signals. San Jose geolocation in the Censys lead is hosting infrastructure, not a victim country.
- Scale and metrics: About 48 hours starting 8 September 2026. Redis operation about 16 hours and 148 actions, failed. GPU-server operation more than 6 hours, about 80 usernames and more than 30,000 credential combinations, failed. MONAI Label path from API mapping to arbitrary file write about 11 minutes, then a root shell in a later session. Two published addresses, records dated 8-10 September 2026. Victim count beyond those operations is unknown.

## Attack flow
### exposed: Exposed Cairn instance (access)

On 10 September 2026 Amazon found a public unauthenticated Cairn instance. Project configs, prompts in Simplified Chinese, and AI output were visible. The campaign had started on 8 September against university addresses in East Asia outside mainland China.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>

### agents: Agents get a shell-access goal (ai)

The operator supplied target addresses and variations of a goal to obtain shell access. Cairn agents planned and adapted without the operator choosing each step. Amazon does not name the model.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>

### failed: Redis and password attempts fail (execution)

About 16 hours and 148 actions against unauthenticated Redis failed, including a Log4Shell attempt, Qdrant SSRF, and credential sprays. A separate GPU-server operation tried more than 30,000 combinations from about 80 public usernames and also failed.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>

### traversal: MONAI Label path traversal (execution)

Agents mapped a medical-imaging AI application's API, found an upload filename parameter, and confirmed path traversal in about 11 minutes. The maintainers' advisory identifies the application as MONAI Label and the endpoints PUT /datastore/image and PUT /datastore/label.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/> <https://github.com/Project-MONAI/MONAILabel/security/advisories/GHSA-qc22-rmgc-vg94>

### shell: Root shell via sitecustomize.py (impact)

A later session wrote a Python reverse shell into sitecustomize.py and triggered a training API endpoint. The new Python process loaded the file and opened a root reverse shell.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>

### second: Proxy into a second university (impact)

The operator then gave the agents a proxy into a second university network whose server ran the same vulnerable software. Amazon infers the zero-day was used to build that tunnel. The agents were directed at other machines there.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>

### Connections

- exposed -> agents
- agents -> failed
- agents -> traversal
- traversal -> shell
- shell -> second

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016.002](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.002) Generative AI — The operator ran Cairn, the same offensive framework mapped on the other Cairn entries, and Amazon reviewed its prompts and AI output.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/> <https://github.com/oritera/Cairn>
- [AML.T0103](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0103) Deploy AI Agent — The operator deployed Cairn agents with target addresses and a goal to obtain shell access, and the agents chose the next steps.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>
- [AML.T0053](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0053) AI Agent Tool Invocation — Amazon says the agents planned, executed, and adapted across Redis, credential spraying, and the MONAI Label upload API without the operator choosing each step.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/>
- [AML.T0049](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0049) Exploit Public-Facing Application — The successful intrusion was path traversal on a reachable MONAI Label datastore upload endpoint, followed by a file write that produced a root shell.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/chinese-nexus-actors-cairn-ai-agents-found-a-zero-day-on-their-own/> <https://github.com/Project-MONAI/MONAILabel/security/advisories/GHSA-qc22-rmgc-vg94>

## Prevention
Do not expose MONAI Label to untrusted networks. The advisory's workarounds are to set MONAI_LABEL_AUTH_ENABLE=True, not to grant ANNOTATOR to untrusted users, and to run the server as an unprivileged user that can write only the datastore directory. The code fix is pull request 1915. The advisory still lists no patched release. Do not leave Cairn panels on 0.0.0.0:8000 without authentication. Alert on writes to sitecustomize.py and on Redis instances that allow unauthenticated access. Block the two published addresses. A same-host-key lead is not an indicator from the Amazon report.

## Detections
- [Python Site or User Customize File Creation](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/linux/persistence_site_and_user_customize_file_creation.toml) (elastic) — Elastic rule on Linux file events. It alerts when sitecustomize.py or usercustomize.py is created. Amazon says a later session wrote a Python reverse shell into sitecustomize.py. Amazon does not name the operating system, so a host that is not covered by this rule's Linux file telemetry will not match. The rule does not detect the MONAI Label upload or the training API call.
- Redis abuse and the failed credential sprays (guidance) — No Sigma, Splunk, or Elastic rule reviewed for this entry names Cairn or the MONAI Label datastore endpoints. Amazon's failed Redis operation used replication abuse, a Lua sandbox escape, configuration writes, memory corruption, a Log4Shell attempt, Qdrant SSRF, and credential sprays against PostgreSQL and SSH. Hunt unauthenticated Redis, unexpected Redis replication, and credential failures from a single source against SSH, PostgreSQL, and campus portals. Those hunts are not a confirmed rule match for this incident.

## Source notes
Campaign dates, the three operations, the root shell, attribution, and the two addresses are from the AWS Security Blog. Amazon's post does not print the product name MONAI Label. BleepingComputer and The Register do, and GHSA-qc22-rmgc-vg94 was reported by aws-sec-vdp and describes the same upload traversal. The advisory's patched-versions field was None on review, even though pull request 1915 is merged. CVE wording is from GHSA-2f43-rwqm-6h67. Amazon cites Gambit Security's report at https://gambit.security/blog-posts/autonomous-ai-agents-online-retailers-25-a-company for a separate financially motivated Cairn case against online retailers. No catalog entry is that report. Link unit42-cairn-cl-sta-1238-2026-11 and cairn-norforge-2026-10. Censys lead, not an IOC. A Censys host snapshot queried at 2026-09-09T12:00:00Z shows 142.4.98[.]145 and 142.4.107[.]34 in AS54600 (PEG TECH INC), geolocated to San Jose, serving an HTTP page titled Cairn on port 8000 (uvicorn) and SSH on port 12686 with the same SSH host key (SHA-256 af825c8d0b6dfc2505b0c59040f8b97effcd37e28bbece0ba693d462fd5e2bce). 142.4.107[.]34 also served a Python SimpleHTTP/0.6 directory listing on port 9999. The listing included cairn-prompts/, 51 files named claude-XXXX-cwd plus a claude-1000 directory, attack1_traversal.snapshot through attack7_pax.snapshot, craft_malicious_snapshot.py, docker_ssrf_probe.sh, docker_exec_rce.py, dns_server.py, dns_callbacks.log, combined_134_passwords.txt, context_passwords.txt, and err_CommonsCollections1.txt and similar err_CommonsCollections names. Whether the claude-XXXX-cwd names are Claude Code working-directory files is not established by the directory listing. A third address, 142.4.107[.]57, presented the same SSH host key in that 9 September snapshot and no Cairn page. It is an unconfirmed lead and is not an IOC. An October snapshot of 142.4.98[.]145 (14 October 2026) no longer showed the Cairn page or port 9999. It showed Apache with PHP 5.6.40 on 8081 and 18082, MySQL 5.6.51 on 33062, and SSH on 12686. The same October check was not repeated here for the other two addresses. No victim IP addresses are included.

# Symantec - Likely AI-written PowerShell toolkit hunts credentials at an African stock exchange (Witchetty implants on the same server)

- ID: symantec-witchetty-african-stock-exchange-ai-toolkit-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-19
- AI role: llm_generated_malware
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/symantec-witchetty-african-stock-exchange-ai-toolkit-2026-11/

## Summary
Thibaut Passilly and Brigid O Gorman (Symantec Threat Hunter Team, Broadcom; SECURITY.COM, created 19 November 2026) describe a likely AI-written or AI-assisted PowerShell toolkit on a stock exchange in an unnamed African country. Forty-one modules ran under a webshell in the IIS worker process for 3 minutes 12 seconds on 20 September 2026, from 08:44 to 08:47 UTC, a new module every few seconds. Host and process modules (spid1, proc2, proc3, smbctx1, smbsc1) enumerated the machine, user, drives, SMB connections, and process trees via WMI. awslocal1 hunted AWS CLI config, environment variables, and Docker, Kubernetes, Terraform, Jenkins, and GitLab processes. iis1, iisa1, and iisp2 profiled IIS through the WebAdministration module. Secret scraping (cfg1, sites1, xpjs1, dep1, sysm1, sysh1, sysv2, gpp1, awsa1) searched web roots, C:\Deployer, and SYSVOL for web.config, .xml, .json, and .env files, Group Policy cpassword values, AWS keys, and database connection strings. rdsnet1 opened TCP 5432 to an AWS RDS PostgreSQL instance. scq1 queried Microsoft SQL Server with integrated authentication. SharePoint and TFS modules (spf1, spl1, spd1, spd2, sps1, tfs1, tfs2) searched sites for the keywords aws, credential, and password and queried TFS project APIs. API modules (api1, api2, map1, dbg1, adm1, admt1, adm2, keys1, key4, xtok1, cscs1, ipo1) probed internal debug endpoints, attempted an admin login with hardcoded credentials to obtain JWTs, and queried profile and banking data. cas1 and wmi1 queried the SCCM AdminService and WMI class SMS_R_System for LastLogonUserName. Harvested x-api-key values and a bearer token were cycled against the exchange investment API and a securities depository gateway. Those requests connected by IP, set the Host header manually, disabled certificate validation, and sent a blank key as a control. cscs1 and ipo1 queried capital-markets endpoints, including one specific investor number that Symantec does not publish. Authorship signals, which Symantec says are not conclusive one by one, are an identical opening line setting ErrorActionPreference to SilentlyContinue, the same outer try/catch, the same hex encoding, the empty-output sentinel 454d505459 (the hex of EMPTY), a DNS exfiltration loop copied into all 41 modules, generic variable names, one module with a unique progress variable, and one deliberate invalid-credential test. Each module split hex output into 50-character chunks and resolved them with [Net.Dns]::GetHostAddresses as a name under the token danoam334iqjo0ap2scg6ofosjxfgqhsa.oast.pro. Symantec calls oast.pro a public out-of-band testing service. The collector was not visible. Symantec does not say whether any credential or key worked. Six persistence implants on the server are attributed to Witchetty (LookingFrog) with high confidence. ESET's April 2022 TA410 profile names LookingFrog as one of three TA410 sub-groups and does not use the name Witchetty. Symantec says one implant was first seen in 2020, that the implants were present on this server in April 2026, and that Symantec's September 2022 reporting had already described Witchetty hitting this same exchange with ProxyShell and ProxyLogon webshells. The toolkit itself is not attributed. Symantec leaves open Witchetty with new tooling, a second operator using the access, or an unrelated actor. No privilege escalation or data theft was seen. The earliest malicious activity Symantec dates on the server is April 2026, and Symantec says the attackers may have been present longer.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Toolkit unattributed. Six persistence implants on the same server are Witchetty (LookingFrog, a TA410 sub-group in ESET's 2022 profile) with high confidence from Symantec. Symantec does not attribute the PowerShell toolkit to Witchetty.

- Actor: Unnamed toolkit. Witchetty implants are separate.
- Country: Unknown
- Motivation: unknown
- Attributed by: Symantec Threat Hunter Team (Broadcom)
- Confidence: low

## AI involvement confidence
vendor-asserted

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Unknown for the 2026 activity. The 41 modules ran under a webshell in the IIS worker process, which Symantec says suggests a vulnerability may have been exploited. Symantec does not name that vulnerability. The 2022 Witchetty access to this exchange used ProxyShell and ProxyLogon. This report does not say those bugs were used again in 2026.
- Victims and sectors: One stock exchange in an unnamed African country. The toolkit ran on an IIS server and queried the exchange investment API, a securities depository gateway, SharePoint, TFS, SCCM, Microsoft SQL Server, and an AWS RDS PostgreSQL endpoint. Symantec does not name the exchange.
- Regions: Africa
- Region notes: Symantec says the victim is a stock exchange in an African country and does not name the country. China, in the Witchetty attribution, is not a victim region. The 2022 activity against two Middle East governments is a different incident and is not given countries in this write-up.
- Scale and metrics: 41 modules in 3 minutes 12 seconds on 20 September 2026, 08:44-08:47 UTC. Earliest malicious activity Symantec dates on the server is April 2026. Six persistence implants, one first seen in 2020. No privilege escalation or data theft observed. Whether any tested credential or key worked is unknown.

## Attack flow
### server: IIS server, access vector unknown (access)

The modules ran under a webshell in the IIS worker. Symantec dates the earliest malicious activity on the server to April 2026 and does not name the 2026 entry vector.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>

### implants: Witchetty implants on the server (execution)

Six persistence implants on the server are attributed to Witchetty with high confidence. One was first seen in 2020. Symantec does not attribute the PowerShell toolkit to that group.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>

### modules: 41 PowerShell modules (ai)

On 20 September 2026, 08:44-08:47 UTC, 41 modules ran in 3 minutes 12 seconds. Symantec reads the shared scaffold as AI-written or AI-assisted and does not identify the model.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>

### hunt: Credential and API tests (execution)

Modules searched web roots, C:\Deployer, and SYSVOL, queried cloud, database, SharePoint, TFS, and SCCM surfaces, and tested keys against the investment API and a securities depository gateway.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>

### exfil: DNS output to oast.pro (impact)

Hex output left as DNS lookups under one oast.pro token. Symantec did not see the collector, privilege escalation, or data theft, and does not know whether any key worked.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>

### Connections

- server -> implants
- server -> modules
- modules -> hunt
- hunt -> exfil

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0102](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0102) Generate Malicious Commands — Symantec assesses the 41 PowerShell modules as AI-written or AI-assisted commands, with a shared scaffold copied into every module.
  Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>
- [AML.T0017](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0017) Develop Capabilities — The toolkit is purpose-built offensive tooling. Symantec's evidence for development is the uniform module scaffold and the unidentified model, not a recovered training pipeline.
  Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>
- [AML.T0050](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0050) Command and Scripting Interpreter — All 41 modules were PowerShell, started under the IIS worker through a webshell.
  Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack>

## Prevention
Alert when the IIS worker spawns PowerShell. Hunt DNS queries for oast.pro and for the published token. Hunt PowerShell that calls [Net.Dns]::GetHostAddresses in a loop, and searches of SYSVOL or web roots for cpassword, web.config, and cloud keys, from the IIS worker. The published file hashes are for the Witchetty implants and related files, not for the 41 modules, which Symantec does not hash. HijackLibs documents the Avast wsc.dll / wsc_proxy.exe pair and the McAfee LockDown.dll / mfeann.exe pair. A Witchetty implant on the host does not by itself identify the author of the PowerShell modules.

## Detections
- [Suspicious Process By Web Server Process](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_webshell_susp_process_spawned_from_webserver.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. A web-server parent, including w3wp.exe, spawning PowerShell or another listed child. Matches the 41 modules running under the IIS worker. It does not identify the module contents or the AI-authorship scaffold.
- [DNS Query to External Service Interaction Domains](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/network/dns/net_dns_external_service_interaction_domains.yml) (sigma) — DNS queries whose name ends in a listed out-of-band domain, including .oast.pro, which the rule labels as ProjectDiscovery interactsh. Symantec's modules resolved names under danoam334iqjo0ap2scg6ofosjxfgqhsa.oast.pro. The rule needs DNS query logs. It does not require the PowerShell \[Net.Dns\]::GetHostAddresses call, and a lookup that never reaches a resolver will not match.
- [Findstr GPP Passwords](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_findstr_gpp_passwords.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation for find.exe or findstr.exe with cpassword, \sysvol\, and .xml on the command line. Symantec's gpp1 module searched SYSVOL for cpassword, but it was PowerShell, not findstr.exe. A PowerShell-only search does not match this rule. No reviewed rule names the 50-character \[Net.Dns\]::GetHostAddresses loop.

## Source notes
Module names, the 20 September 2026 window, authorship signals, the oast.pro token, the Witchetty split, and the file hashes are from the Symantec article at https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/african-stock-exchange-ai-assisted-attack. The page's created timestamp is 19 November 2026 11:00 UTC. The blog-post URL serves the same article. Authors are printed as Thibaut Passilly and Brigid O Gorman. Symantec calls oast.pro a public out-of-band testing service and does not name ProjectDiscovery or interactsh in the article text reviewed here. The Sigma DNS rule cited below lists .oast.pro among ProjectDiscovery interactsh domains. ESET's 2022 post names LookingFrog as a TA410 sub-group and does not use the name Witchetty. HijackLibs lists Avast wsc.dll loaded by wsc_proxy.exe and McAfee LockDown.dll loaded by mfeann.exe. No HijackLibs page for VSPerfCmd.exe or VSPMsg.dll was found, and a guessed path for that pair returned HTTP 404, so that pair is not linked. PowerShell is not a LOLBAS entry. The hash list below is the named artifacts from Symantec's table that this entry describes, not every hash on the page. Hashes marked not available for analysis, and unnamed similar svchost loaders, are omitted. The domain is defanged. No victim IP addresses were published.

# GTIG finds an exposed exploit_agent server that diffs patches and runs exploits

- ID: gtig-exploit-agent-staging-server-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-16
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/gtig-exploit-agent-staging-server-2026-11/

## Summary
Google Threat Intelligence Group and Mandiant (Aurora Blum, Kelli Vanderlee, Jess Xia, and David Mainor, 16 November 2026) say that in the past quarter GTIG identified threat actors using LLM frameworks to automate vulnerability discovery and exploitation without a human in the loop. One case was an exposed staging server hosting a multi-agent vulnerability research and weaponization framework named exploit_agent. It integrates with multiple frontier and local LLMs. The framework continuously diffs popular web-application software, comparing code before and after patch announcements, analyzes the diff for vulnerabilities, drafts exploits, and executes them without human intervention. On the server, with data exfiltrated from targeted victims, GTIG found scripts for internal reconnaissance, brute-forcing, installing webshells, and removing other attackers' webshells. Victim telemetry on the server indicated interest in French websites in media and entertainment, and in education. A published sample shows the framework inspecting access-control code in the Spectra (Ultimate Addons for Gutenberg) WordPress plugin, version 2.19.25. An environment-file comment, in Russian, labels LLM API keys for auto-exploitation and tells the operator to write the keys in that file rather than in chat. GTIG does not name the operator, a country, or a public repository for exploit_agent. The Russian comment is an artifact on the server, not a stated victim or actor country.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed. GTIG and Mandiant identified the exposed server. No cluster name and no country are stated.

- Actor: Unnamed operator
- Motivation: unknown
- Attributed by: Google Threat Intelligence Group and Mandiant
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Exposed staging server. For the victim sites, the report says the framework drafts and runs exploits against web applications. It does not name the vulnerability used at each site.
- Victims and sectors: French websites in media and entertainment, and in education. No organization is named.
- Regions: France
- Region notes: France is the victim-website geography GTIG stated. The Russian-language environment comment is an artifact on the staging server, not a victim region and not an attributed actor country.
- Scale and metrics: One exposed staging server. Victim telemetry indicated French media, entertainment, and education sites. No victim count was published.

## Attack flow
### staging: Exposed exploit_agent staging server (access)

GTIG found a staging server hosting the multi-agent framework and data taken from victims.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### diff: Agents diff patches and draft exploits (ai)

The framework compares web-application code across patch releases, looks for vulnerabilities, and drafts exploits with frontier and local LLMs. A sample inspection target is Spectra 2.19.25.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### run: Framework runs the exploits (execution)

GTIG says the framework executes the drafted exploits without a human in the loop.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### post: Webshell, recon, and brute-force scripts (execution)

Scripts on the server install webshells, remove other attackers' webshells, perform internal reconnaissance, and brute-force.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### victims: French media, entertainment, and education (impact)

Victim telemetry on the server pointed at French websites in those sectors. No organization is named.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### Connections

- staging -> diff
- diff -> run
- run -> post
- post -> victims

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016.001](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.001) Software Tools — GTIG found a deployed multi-agent software framework, exploit_agent, built to research and weaponize web-application patches.
  Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

## Prevention
Treat a short patch window on internet-facing web applications as the defensive problem this report describes. Review Spectra (Ultimate Addons for Gutenberg) 2.19.25 and later plugin updates, unexpected PHP writes under plugin directories, webshell install and removal, reconnaissance and brute-force scripts on web servers, and outbound use of newly captured data. No public exploit_agent repository was identified.

## Detections
- [Web Server Potential Command Injection Request](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/cross-platform/persistence_web_server_potential_command_injection.toml) (elastic) — Matched webshell and web command execution with security-detections-mcp. HTTP requests whose URLs contain command-execution patterns. GTIG says exploit_agent drafts and runs exploits against web applications. The rule does not name exploit_agent or Spectra.
- [PHP File Creation in WordPress Plugin Directory](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/linux/persistence_webserver_php_file_creation_in_wordpress_plugin_dir.toml) (elastic) — Matched WordPress with security-detections-mcp. A PHP file written under a WordPress plugin directory by a web server, PHP handler, shell, or download tool. The published sample is the framework reading Spectra 2.19.25, a WordPress plugin, and the server also held webshell-install scripts. GTIG did not say a PHP file was written into a plugin directory, and did not name the operating system. This is the closest WordPress rule in the index.
- [Linux Webshell Indicators](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/linux/process_creation/proc_creation_lnx_webshell_detection.yml) (sigma) — Matched webshell with security-detections-mcp. A web-server process starting a shell or reconnaissance command. GTIG describes webshell install and removal plus reconnaissance scripts. The rule is Linux process creation. The report does not name Linux, Windows, or the commands.
- No rule names exploit_agent or the brute-force scripts (guidance) — security-detections-mcp searches for exploit_agent, Spectra, and brute force did not return a rule for this framework or for an unnamed web login. Brute-force rules in the index were for Okta, NTLM, RDP, SSH, or AWS IAM. None of those is the web-application brute-force script GTIG describes. The Russian environment-file comment is an operator note about where to put LLM API keys, not a host event a public rule encodes.

## Timeline
- Last updated: 2026-11-16
- 2026-11-16 (Google Threat Intelligence Group and Mandiant): GTIG publishes the exposed exploit_agent staging server, including unattended patch diffing and exploit execution, webshell and brute-force scripts, French media, entertainment, and education targeting, and the Spectra 2.19.25 sample. No country and no public repository are stated.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- exploit_agent multi-agent framework
- Frontier and local LLMs, models not named

### Other tooling
- Scripts for reconnaissance, brute force, webshell install, and webshell removal

## Source notes
The server, the exploit_agent name, the no-human-in-the-loop diff-and-execute loop, the post-compromise scripts, the French sector targeting, the Spectra 2.19.25 sample, and the Russian environment-file comment are from the 16 November 2026 GTIG tracker. The Russian text is quoted there as an LLM API-key comment for the exploit_agent pilot. It is not used as a country. GTIG published no repository URL. Searches during this review did not find a public exploit_agent project to link. No IP addresses, domains, or file hashes were published for this server.

## Sources
- [GTIG AI Threat Tracker: Agentic Threat Activity Crosses a Significant Threshold](https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/) — Google Threat Intelligence Group

# Suspected MIDNIGHT NEPTUNE uses SynthID recruiter lures and Claude Code settings to deploy SOMBERMEME

- ID: gtig-midnight-neptune-claude-settings-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-16
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/gtig-midnight-neptune-claude-settings-2026-11/

## Summary
Google Threat Intelligence Group (Aurora Blum, Kelli Vanderlee, Jess Xia, and David Mainor, 16 November 2026) describes suspected MIDNIGHT NEPTUNE activity in the DPRK section of the quarterly tracker. In recent intrusions against cryptocurrency organizations in North America and Europe, the cluster used LLM-enabled social engineering and the victim organization's AI tools as an internal supply-chain vector. For access, the actor created fraudulent recruiter profiles on professional networking platforms. The profile images contained SynthID watermarks, which GTIG says confirms LLM-synthesized identities. The personas sent targets to fraudulent meeting platforms with ClickFix lures that asked them to run diagnostic fix scripts. GTIG calls those staging scripts likely LLM-authored. After the initial compromise, the actor changed .claude/settings.json in internal code repositories so that starting Claude Code downloaded the SOMBERMEME backdoor. The actor also created GitHub personal access tokens named after Microsoft Copilot. GTIG places this in a broader observation that DPRK operators use generative tools for scale and use native agent execution hooks for enterprise access. No IP addresses, hashes, domains, or public tool repository were published for this cluster.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Suspected MIDNIGHT NEPTUNE. GTIG describes the activity under Democratic People's Republic of Korea threat actors. The cluster name and the country are both stated as suspected.

- Actor: Suspected MIDNIGHT NEPTUNE
- Country: North Korea
- Motivation: nation-state
- Attributed by: Google Threat Intelligence Group
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Fraudulent recruiter profiles on professional networking platforms, then ClickFix lures on fraudulent meeting pages that ask the target to run a diagnostic fix script.
- Victims and sectors: Cryptocurrency organizations in North America and Europe. No organization is named.
- Regions: North America, Europe
- Region notes: North America and Europe are the target regions GTIG named. North Korea is the suspected actor country, not a victim region.
- Scale and metrics: GTIG says multiple intrusions over the course of 2026 used this combination, and describes recent intrusions against cryptocurrency organizations in North America and Europe. No victim count was published.

## Attack flow
### recruit: SynthID recruiter profiles (access)

Fraudulent recruiter profiles on professional networking platforms use images that carry SynthID watermarks.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### clickfix: ClickFix on a fake meeting page (execution)

The personas direct targets to fraudulent meeting platforms. ClickFix pages ask them to run diagnostic fix scripts that GTIG calls likely LLM-authored.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### settings: Claude Code settings.json is changed (ai)

In internal repositories, .claude/settings.json is modified so that launching Claude Code downloads SOMBERMEME.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### somber: Claude Code downloads SOMBERMEME (impact)

Starting Claude Code triggers the download of the SOMBERMEME backdoor.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### pats: GitHub PATs named after Copilot (execution)

The actor creates GitHub personal access tokens named after Microsoft Copilot.

Evidence: <https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/>

### Connections

- recruit -> clickfix
- clickfix -> settings
- settings -> somber
- somber -> pats

## MITRE ATLAS
No ATLAS mapping is recorded.

## Prevention
Review professional-network recruiter approaches that lead to a meeting page and a pasted diagnostic script. Diff .claude/settings.json in internal repositories, and alert when a process rewrites a file under .claude/ before Claude Code starts a download. Review new GitHub personal access tokens, including tokens whose names refer to Microsoft Copilot. No SOMBERMEME hash or domain was published.

## Detections
- [Suspicious ClickFix/FileFix Execution Pattern](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_susp_clickfix_filefix_execution.yml) (sigma) — Matched ClickFix with security-detections-mcp. Windows process creation under explorer.exe for the Run-dialog or File Explorer paste pattern, often mshta.exe or powershell.exe. GTIG named ClickFix and diagnostic fix scripts. It did not publish the command or the operating system, so this rule applies only where that Windows paste path was used.
- [Unusual Process Modifying GenAI Configuration File](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/cross-platform/defense_evasion_genai_config_modification.toml) (elastic) — Matched settings.json and .claude with security-detections-mcp. A file modification under .claude/, which includes .claude/settings.json, by an unusual process. GTIG describes that file being changed in internal repositories. The rule sees a host file write, not the git commit by itself.
- [New GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT) Added](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/github/persistence_new_pat_created.toml) (elastic) — Matched personal access token with security-detections-mcp. GitHub audit creation of a PAT. GTIG says the tokens were named after Microsoft Copilot. The rule does not match on that name.
- No rule names SOMBERMEME or SynthID (guidance) — security-detections-mcp searches for SOMBERMEME, SynthID, and deepfake returned no Sigma, Splunk, or Elastic rule. SynthID is a watermark on the published profile images, not a host event. SOMBERMEME has no published hash or domain in this report to attach to a malware rule.

## Timeline
- Last updated: 2026-11-16
- 2026-11-16 (Google Threat Intelligence Group): GTIG describes suspected MIDNIGHT NEPTUNE intrusions against cryptocurrency organizations in North America and Europe, using SynthID recruiter images, likely LLM-authored ClickFix scripts, a .claude/settings.json change that makes Claude Code download SOMBERMEME, and GitHub PATs named after Microsoft Copilot.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- LLM image generation, evidenced by SynthID watermarks
- Likely LLM-authored ClickFix staging scripts
- [Claude Code](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code), via .claude/settings.json

### Other tooling
- SOMBERMEME backdoor
- GitHub personal access tokens named after Microsoft Copilot

## Source notes
The cluster name, SynthID watermarks, ClickFix meeting pages, the likely LLM-authored scripts, the .claude/settings.json change, SOMBERMEME, the Copilot-named GitHub tokens, and the North America and Europe cryptocurrency targeting are from the 16 November 2026 GTIG tracker. Category is adversary-use-of-ai because the operator used generative models for the recruiter imagery and, GTIG says, likely for the staging scripts. The change to the victim's Claude Code settings is ai_role_secondary ai_agent_or_mcp_as_target. That split matches other entries that contain both an operator role and abuse of an AI tool, and the category order in scripts/import-notion.mjs. No frameworks value is set. GTIG did not name an offensive framework for this cluster, and did not publish a command line or a host platform for the ClickFix script.

## Sources
- [GTIG AI Threat Tracker: Agentic Threat Activity Crosses a Significant Threshold](https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/agentic-threat-activity-crosses-a-significant-threshold/) — Google Threat Intelligence Group

# Symantec - AI-assisted commands revived a stalled intrusion at a US retailer

- ID: symantec-ai-revived-retailer-intrusion-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-16
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/symantec-ai-revived-retailer-intrusion-2026-11/

## Summary
Symantec Threat Hunter Team (Broadcom; SECURITY.COM article created Nov 16, 2026) describes a September 2026 intrusion at a U.S. retailer. The initial access vector is unknown. The earliest activity, Sep 18, was on three Microsoft SQL Server hosts, with cmd.exe running under sqlservr.exe, which Symantec treats as likely xp_cmdshell. Some of those commands were already long PowerShell scripts with quoted section labels. The scripts searched point-of-sale synchronization directories for stored passwords and connection strings, tested egress to 155.254.22[.]215:9458, and on one host appended base64 text under ProgramData, decoded it with Certutil, and compiled it with csc.exe. The source was EfsPotato (https://github.com/zcgonvh/EfsPotato). The payload saved the SAM, SYSTEM, and SECURITY hives. On Sep 21 an internet-facing IIS server appeared, with commands under w3wp.exe. On Sep 22 the commands looked hand-typed: 110 actions, three typos including systemifo and pwoershell, average length about 45 characters. Loaders were killed by security software, including a Bitdefender bdreinit.exe sideload of log.dll and log.txt from 204.194.49[.]129:8080, a pairing also seen with ShadowPad loaders and not unique to one actor. The operators reran that loader about five times in 20 minutes. They also staged an AES PowerShell reverse shell, server.ps1, toward 166.0.25[.]106:443 with a fallback host at 154.81.37[.]136, and wrote ASP webshells filemanager.asp and qwdajhsd.asp. On Sep 23 they staged a G DATA ask.exe/ask.dll sideload and BreakChainC.exe, then switched to about 50 long, error-free PowerShell scripts over about two hours. Banners included ===FILES MENTIONING CONN STRINGS===. Comments stated a hypothesis, cited earlier results, and revised a conclusion, which Symantec reads as model reasoning pasted into a webshell. The scripts collected connection strings, the FileZilla Server user database, WinSCP, PuTTY, and RDP settings, IIS configuration, and Windows logon registry values. They found SolarWinds Orion, read its bundled RabbitMQ .erlang.cookie, and ran Orion's erl.exe so rpc:call reached os:cmd on a second server. The command was passed as decimal character codes. That was about 10 minutes after the first Orion query. Later the same day BreakChainCs.exe relaunched the sideloaded binaries through WMI and shell modes so they were no longer children of w3wp.exe. From Sep 24 to Sep 29 the activity reached more SQL servers, a host that looks like a domain controller, Chisel, a password sprayer, and searches for NTDS.dit, Group Policy Preferences passwords, and LAPS. No data theft or encryption was observed. Symantec's financial-motive reading rests on the point-of-sale password search, contact with 155.254.22[.]215 (named in a separate Sep 22 Gambit Security report as an AI console tied to card-skimming activity), and later domain-credential theft that could be ransomware preparation. Loader files and the staging server overlap an August intrusion elsewhere and a July intrusion at a securities firm; neither of those showed AI assistance. Yuze (https://github.com/P001water/yuze) was used in the August case back to 204.194.49[.]129. Trend Micro has documented Yuze use by Warlock operators (Symantec Longlegs, also known as Storm-2603). Symantec does not attribute this intrusion to them. ATT&CK techniques in the report include T1574.002, T1505.003, T1552.001, T1021, and T1003.002.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed. Symantec does not tie the activity to Warlock, Longlegs, or Storm-2603. ShadowPad-associated sideloading and Yuze are shared with other actors and are not treated as an attribution.

- Actor: Unnamed
- Country: Unknown
- Motivation: financially-motivated
- Attributed by: Symantec Threat Hunter Team (Broadcom)
- Confidence: low

## AI involvement confidence
vendor-asserted

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Unknown. The first observed commands were cmd.exe under sqlservr.exe on SQL Server, consistent with xp_cmdshell, and later cmd.exe under w3wp.exe on IIS.
- Victims and sectors: A U.S. retail organization. Internet-facing IIS and at least six further servers, most of them Microsoft SQL Server. Point-of-sale synchronization scripts were searched for passwords.
- Regions: United States
- Region notes: The primary victim is a U.S. retailer. The August and July overlaps are different organizations and are not given countries in the retailer write-up.
- Scale and metrics: Sep 18 through at least Sep 29, 2026. On Sep 23, about 50 scripts in about two hours, median length over 500 characters, against about 45 characters for the earlier hand-typed commands. About 10 minutes from the first Orion query to command execution on a second server. No theft or encryption observed in this intrusion.

## Attack flow
### sql-and-iis: SQL Server and IIS command shells (access)

From Sep 18, cmd.exe runs under sqlservr.exe. From Sep 21, commands run under w3wp.exe. Symantec does not identify the initial entry vector.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>

### stalled: Hand-typed commands stall (execution)

Sep 22 commands include typos such as systemifo and pwoershell. Security software kills staged loaders, including the bdreinit.exe and log.dll pair, and the operators retry without changing approach.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>

### ai-scripts: Long labelled scripts (ai)

On Sep 23 about 50 error-free PowerShell scripts run in about two hours, with section banners and comments Symantec reads as model reasoning. Pauses fit an operator pasting the next script.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>

### orion-move: Orion cookie reaches a second server (impact)

About 10 minutes after the first Orion query, the operators read the RabbitMQ cookie and use the bundled erl.exe rpc:call path to run a command on a second server. Later activity dumps credential hives. No encryption was observed.

Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>

### Connections

- sql-and-iis -> stalled
- stalled -> ai-scripts
- ai-scripts -> orion-move

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016) Obtain Capabilities — Symantec says the operators obtained working PowerShell, including the Orion cookie handoff, from an AI tool after hand-typed commands failed.
  Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>
- [AML.T0050](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0050) Command and Scripting Interpreter — The AI-assisted phase was long PowerShell submitted through the webshell and through SQL Server command execution.
  Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>
- [AML.T0055](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0055) Unsecured Credentials — The scripts collected connection strings, client settings, the Erlang cookie, and registry hives that hold credential material.
  Evidence: <https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer>

## Prevention
Alert when w3wp.exe or sqlservr.exe spawns cmd.exe or PowerShell, and when xp_cmdshell is used. Treat a same-directory unsigned DLL next to bdreinit.exe or ask.exe as sideloading. Alert on csc.exe compiling from ProgramData or a user-writable path, on Chisel client arguments, and on reg save of SAM, SYSTEM, or SECURITY. Hunt Orion's bundled erl.exe making rpc:call with a cookie, and ASP files dropped in the web application's image directory. A sudden change from short mistyped commands to long labelled scripts is an investigation lead, not a benign automation signature. Restrict xp_cmdshell, the Erlang distribution port, and the RabbitMQ cookie file.

## Detections
- [Suspicious Process By Web Server Process](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_webshell_susp_process_spawned_from_webserver.yml) (sigma) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for w3wp. Windows process creation. A web-server parent, including w3wp.exe, spawning a suspicious child. Matches commands Symantec saw under the IIS worker. It does not match sqlservr.exe as the parent.
- [Suspicious Child Process Of SQL Server](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_mssql_susp_child_process.yml) (sigma) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for sqlservr. Windows process creation. sqlservr.exe spawning cmd.exe, PowerShell, or another listed child. Matches the Sep 18 pattern Symantec reads as xp_cmdshell.
- [MSSQL XPCmdshell Suspicious Execution](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/builtin/application/mssqlserver/win_mssql_xp_cmdshell_audit_log.yml) (sigma) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for xp_cmdshell. Windows application log. MSSQL audit event 33205 whose data contains object_name:xp_cmdshell and statement:EXEC. Requires the xp_cmdshell audit policy. The process-creation rule above still applies when that audit policy is off.
- [Windows Unsigned DLL Side-Loading In Same Process Path](https://github.com/splunk/security_content/blob/develop/detections/endpoint/windows_unsigned_dll_side_loading_in_same_process_path.yml) (splunk) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for DLL side-loading. Sysmon image load. An unsigned DLL loaded from the same directory as the process, outside Windows and Program Files. Fits bdreinit.exe with log.dll and ask.exe with ask.dll staged in the same folder. A signed decoy executable does not have to be on a named-binary list.
- [Privilege Escalation via Rogue Named Pipe Impersonation](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/windows/privilege_escalation_via_rogue_named_pipe.toml) (elastic) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for Potato. The rule cites https://github.com/zcgonvh/EfsPotato and alerts on Sysmon pipe-creation event 17 when the pipe path contains a nested Pipe segment. It does not match the string EfsPotato. The Splunk Potato-family rule's file-name list does not include EfsPotato, so that rule is not cited. A renamed compile can still miss a name-based hunt.
- [Dynamic .NET Compilation Via Csc.EXE](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_csc_susp_dynamic_compilation.yml) (sigma) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for csc.exe. Windows process creation. csc.exe whose command line points at ProgramData, Temp, or another listed user-writable location. Symantec compiled under ProgramData. The rule filters a w3wp.exe parent, so a compile whose parent is only the IIS worker does not match.
- [PUA - Chisel Tunneling Tool Execution](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_pua_chisel.yml) (sigma) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search for chisel. Windows process creation. Image ending in chisel.exe, or command lines with client/server and reverse, socks, or r: arguments. Symantec's Chisel detection on Sep 25 was on a Windows host. The Linux Chisel rule is not cited.
- [Windows Registry Entries Exported Via Reg](https://github.com/splunk/security_content/blob/develop/detections/endpoint/windows_registry_entries_exported_via_reg.yml) (splunk) — Selected from a security-detections-mcp search under T1003.002. Endpoint process data. reg.exe with save or export. Symantec says the EfsPotato payload saved the SAM, SYSTEM, and SECURITY hives. This matches that copy when it is reg.exe. A custom writer that does not call reg.exe will not match.
- Erlang rpc:call and the Orion cookie (guidance) — security-detections-mcp search for erl.exe returned no rule. Nothing in the index alerts on Orion's bundled erl.exe, rpc:call, or a read of .erlang.cookie. Hunt process creation for erl.exe under the Orion Erlang path with -setcookie and rpc:call. ASP names filemanager.asp and qwdajhsd.asp, and BreakChainC.exe, are also absent from the index; the w3wp child-process rule covers shells spawned from the web worker, not the ASP file drop by itself.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-09-18
- Last updated: 2026-11-16
- 2026-09-18 (Symantec Threat Hunter Team): First activity on three SQL Server hosts. Labelled PowerShell searches point-of-sale sync folders, tests egress to 155.254.22\[.\]215:9458, and compiles EfsPotato on one host.
- 2026-09-21 (Symantec Threat Hunter Team): Commands start under w3wp.exe on the internet-facing web server, then pause for about 12 hours.
- 2026-09-22 (Symantec Threat Hunter Team): Hand-typed commands with typos. bdreinit.exe/log.dll from 204.194.49\[.\]129:8080 is killed on each retry. server.ps1 and two ASP webshells are staged.
- 2026-09-23 (Symantec Threat Hunter Team): About 50 long scripts in about two hours. Orion's Erlang cookie is used to run a command on a second server roughly 10 minutes after the first Orion query. BreakChainCs.exe follows.
- 2026-09-24 (Symantec Threat Hunter Team): Further SQL hosts save registry hives. Activity continues through at least Sep 29, including Chisel, NTDS.dit searches, and a password sprayer.
- 2026-11-16 (Symantec Threat Hunter Team): SECURITY.COM publishes the retailer write-up. AI assistance is high confidence; the operator-in-the-loop reading is moderate; the model is unidentified.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- Unidentified model. Symantec assesses AI-written [PowerShell](https://github.com/PowerShell/PowerShell) with an operator relaying output, not an autonomous agent.

### Other tooling
- EfsPotato (https://github.com/zcgonvh/EfsPotato), compiled on the server with csc.exe
- Chisel (https://github.com/jpillora/chisel)
- Yuze (https://github.com/P001water/yuze), seen in the overlapping August intrusion
- Bitdefender bdreinit.exe sideloading log.dll
- G DATA ask.exe sideloading ask.dll
- BreakChainC.exe and BreakChainCs.exe
- ASP webshells filemanager.asp and qwdajhsd.asp
- [PowerShell](https://github.com/PowerShell/PowerShell)

## Source notes
Dates, typos, script counts, the Orion cookie path, the decimal-encoded rpc:call, and the published hashes are from the Symantec article at https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/ai-assisted-attack-us-retailer (canonical; the blog-post URL serves the same title). The article's createdDate is 2026-11-16. Symantec lists many additional file hashes; the set below is the named tooling from that table, not every unnamed suspicious file. Yuze's Warlock reporting is Symantec's summary of Trend Micro, and Symantec says it is not an attribution. Link the August staging overlap only as Symantec describes it.

# Dynatrace mcpvenom measures MCP tool-description poisoning across 12 frameworks

- ID: dynatrace-mcpvenom-tool-poisoning-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-15
- AI role: ai_agent_or_mcp_as_target
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/dynatrace-mcpvenom-tool-poisoning-2026-11/

## Summary
Farooq Shaikh and Biagio Montaruli (Dynatrace; AISec at ACM CCS 2026, Nov 15-19, The Hague) measure MCP tool poisoning with real tool execution. mcpvenom (https://github.com/dynatrace-research/mcpvenom) runs 45 scenarios across 12 frameworks and 18 models, 9,720 cells. The attacker controls one MCP server the user installed and can change tool descriptions, input-schema descriptions, and tool responses. The attacker cannot change the system prompt, user messages, framework code, or model weights. A legitimate server exposes read_file, write_file, list_files, http_get, and http_post. A planted secret sits in the workspace. A canary HTTP server on port 18080 records exfiltration. Overall, 2,113 cells were vulnerable (22.9 percent). Of those, 64.9 percent were a dispatched attacker tool call, 23.0 percent were confirmed network contact, and 12.1 percent were a verified secret in the canary log. Hand review covered 1,259 transcripts. Nine core frameworks clustered between 23 and 26 percent, and the paper says none of the 12 implements an intentional description check. Pydantic AI's duplicate-tool-name rejection landed at 16.6 percent, and AG2's executor constraints at 4.6 percent. A regex scanner the authors built rejected 20 of 45 scenarios and flagged 9 of 125 benign descriptions (7.2 percent). Model results ran from Claude Fable 5 at 0.4 percent to Amazon Nova Pro at 49.7 percent (245 of 493 cells). Claude Opus 4.8 was vulnerable in 8 of 530 cells (1.5 percent) and issued 223 refusals that cited the poisoned description. Claude Sonnet 5 sometimes named the text as prompt injection and then made the exfiltration call. The simplest high-signal payload appends an instruction to read /workspace/.env and send it with http_post. That direct-description class scored 29.2 percent aggregate ASR outside AG2. A language-switch payload scored 66.0 percent. Five disclosures to four organizations were rejected: two to LangChain, one to Microsoft for AutoGen via MSRC, one to the MCP SDK, and one to Haystack (deepset). The AutoGen rejection is about microsoft/autogen, which the paper says is not the AG2 0.14.0 fork in the matrix. Link invariant-mcp-tool-poisoning-2025-04, the earlier description-poisoning report this paper cites.

Evidence qualification: This is a lab measurement with planted secrets and a canary server. The paper does not describe an intrusion against a named organization. All five vendor disclosures were rejected.

## Category
attacks-on-ai

## Attribution
No threat actor. Dynatrace Research ran the measurement. The five vendor reports were rejected.

- Actor: unknown
- Motivation: unknown
- Attributed by: Dynatrace
- Confidence: low

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: The user installs an MCP server whose tool description, schema text, or tool response carries the instruction. The paper's servers run over stdio in the lab.
- Victims and sectors: No customer is named. The matrix uses planted workspace secrets and framework harnesses.
- Regions: Not applicable
- Region notes: The Hague is the workshop location, not a victim region. Downloads in the paper's framework table are as of May 2026 and are not a victim count.
- Scale and metrics: 9,720 cells. 2,113 vulnerable (22.9 percent), including 255 cells (12.1 percent of the vulnerable set) with a canary receipt of the secret. 45 scenarios. 12 frameworks. 18 models.

## Attack flow
### install: Malicious MCP server is installed (access)

The user adds a server the attacker published. It is registered next to a legitimate server that can read files and call http_post.

Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>

### describe: Poisoned description enters the prompt (ai)

Frameworks copy tool descriptions, schema text, and tool responses into the model context. The paper says none of the 12 checks that text on purpose.

Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>

### post: http_post carries the secret (execution)

A typical payload tells the model to read /workspace/.env and send it with the legitimate http_post tool.

Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>

### canary: Canary confirms exfiltration (impact)

255 of the vulnerable cells put the planted secret on the canary at port 18080. The full vulnerable set is 2,113 of 9,720 cells.

Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>

### Connections

- install -> describe
- describe -> post
- post -> canary

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0051.001](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0051.001) Indirect — Tool descriptions, schema text, and tool responses are attacker-controlled fields copied into the model prompt
  Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>
- [AML.T0053](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0053) AI Agent Tool Invocation — The successful cells invoke a legitimate tool, most clearly http_post, because the poisoned description told the model to
  Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>
- [AML.T0055](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0055) Unsecured Credentials — The verified tier is a planted workspace secret received by the canary HTTP server
  Evidence: <https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555>

## Prevention
Review an MCP server's tool descriptions before adding it, and treat a description that names another tool, a file such as .env, or an outbound URL as hostile. Do not assume the framework checks that text. The paper's own regex scanner missed 25 of 45 scenarios, so a description scan is only a partial control. Prefer a model that refused in this matrix, and still review the server, because the paper says no framework defense made up for a susceptible model.

## Detections
- Lab tool-description poisoning has no product rule (guidance) — The paper's signal is a tool description that instructs a read of .env and an http_post, then a request to a canary on port 18080. No Sigma, Splunk, or Elastic rule names MCP tool descriptions. Review descriptions at install time. The authors' regex scanner rejected 20 of 45 scenarios and flagged 9 of 125 benign descriptions, so a scan is not coverage by itself.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-11-15
- Last updated: 2026-11-15
- 2026-11-15 (Dynatrace): AISec workshop paper, Nov 15-19, 2026. mcpvenom results: 22.9 percent of 9,720 cells vulnerable, and five vendor disclosures rejected.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- mcpvenom (https://github.com/dynatrace-research/mcpvenom)
- 12 MCP frameworks, including LangChain 1.5.0, Pydantic AI 2.14.1, CrewAI 1.15.5, Semantic Kernel 1.44.0, Haystack 3.0.0, and AG2 0.14.0
- 18 models, from [Claude](https://claude.com/product/overview) Fable 5 (0.4 percent) to Amazon Nova Pro (49.7 percent)

### Other tooling
- Legitimate http_post tool used to send the planted secret
- Canary HTTP server on port 18080

### MagicSword projects
- [LOLSkills](https://lolskills.io)

## Source notes
Counts, model rates, framework versions, the http_post payload, the canary port, and the five rejections are from the paper text at https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555. The workshop dates are Nov 15-19, 2026. The paper does not give a separate preprint day, so date_reported is the workshop start. Link invariant-mcp-tool-poisoning-2025-04.

## Sources
- [MCP Tool Poisoning in Agentic AI Frameworks: A Cross-Framework Measurement Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555) — AISec, ACM CCS 2026
- [mcpvenom](https://github.com/dynatrace-research/mcpvenom) — Dynatrace Research

# Amazon MadPot records ARTEX agents hunting AI provider keys and attempting exchange KYC deepfakes

- ID: amazon-artex-madpot-campaigns-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-13
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/amazon-artex-madpot-campaigns-2026-11/

## Summary
Amazon Threat Intelligence (CJ Moses, AWS Security Blog, 13 November 2026) says that from 3 October to 5 November 2026, MadPot decoys configured as popular LLM chat-completion APIs received requests from AI agents. The requests carried persistent task context, system prompts, tool histories, knowledge-graph state, and reported findings. Amazon assesses with moderate confidence that the agents ran on ARTEX, from prompt references to ARTEX tooling and other ARTEX artifacts, and with moderate confidence that the operators were disparate Chinese-nexus groups, from simplified Chinese prompts, China time-zone settings, targeting consistent with Chinese interests, and tooling predominantly used by Chinese-nexus threats. Standing instructions set mission, targeting, and constraints, including a fixed country sequence, a preference for enterprise production sites over personal projects, a ban on mainland China targets and government domains, and a requirement that outbound traffic use proxies. Credentials exposed for more than 30 days were to be treated as low-value. Most activity split work across planner, worker, verification, and compression agents sharing a knowledge graph. Campaign 1, in the agents' records, hunted internet-exposed AI gateways and application builders, extracted upstream provider keys, validated them, and pooled them. Named targets were One API, New API, Veloera, Sub2API, LiteLLM, Dify, FastGPT, Langflow, RAGFlow, Open WebUI, LobeChat, and Ollama. The recorded playbook mapped each product to known chains, including uninitialized installs, anonymous management and provider-configuration endpoints, the LiteLLM default master key, CVE-2025-3248 (Langflow), CVE-2026-28797 (RAGFlow), an isCliRequest authentication bypass on One API family systems, SSRF through URL-fetch features to cloud metadata services, and secrets in client-side JavaScript, source maps, debug routes, and environment files. Discovery used internet scanning, certificate transparency, GitHub repositories and event archives, npm and PyPI tarballs, container layers, notebooks, model-hosting spaces, paste sites, and Telegram. Stolen keys were fed into the framework gateway so stolen inference funded more hunting. Cloud credentials were validated, storage was enumerated, and managed AI services, secrets stores, and application identities were prioritized over general cloud permissions. Leads were graded L1, L2 for a working API call, and L3 for quantified impact. Campaign 2, in concurrent sessions against two cryptocurrency exchanges, attempted real-time deepfake video for liveness checks and forged ID-document photos. The agents' records say the vendor's server-side verdict rejected the deepfake on every recorded attempt. Amazon did not say whether the forged document photos passed. At least four sessions ran at once, on cash-out, balances, the deepfake pipeline, and the verification bypass. Amazon did not observe the described activity on other organizations as it happened and cannot verify the outcomes the agents reported. It notified potentially affected AWS customers. Community forks that answered HTTP 200 on 16 November 2026 are https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX and https://github.com/MoLeft/ARTEX. The original Autumn-27/ARTEX repository returned HTTP 404.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed Chinese-nexus groups. Amazon Threat Intelligence, moderate confidence, from simplified Chinese prompts, China time zones, targeting, and tooling. Financially motivated. Not a single named cluster.

- Actor: Unnamed Chinese-nexus groups
- Country: China
- Motivation: financially-motivated
- Attributed by: Amazon Threat Intelligence
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: The agents' records describe exposed AI management interfaces, default credentials, known product flaws, and public artifact searches. Amazon did not observe those intrusions on customer or third-party systems.
- Victims and sectors: Agents' records describe hundreds of targets across AI application platforms and other workloads, plus two cryptocurrency exchanges. Amazon notified potentially affected AWS customers. No organization is named here.
- Regions: unknown
- Region notes: Amazon describes a fixed country sequence and an instruction to exclude mainland China and government domains. It does not name the countries of the hundreds of targets or of the two exchanges. Those instructions are not recorded as victim regions.
- Scale and metrics: MadPot collection window 3 October through 5 November 2026. The records describe hundreds of targets. Campaign 2 names two cryptocurrency exchanges and at least four concurrent agent sessions. Every recorded liveness verdict rejected the deepfake. No confirmed victim count was published.

## Attack flow
### instruct: Standing instructions set the mission (access)

Operators gave ARTEX agents objectives, a country sequence, proxy rules, and grading for stolen credentials. The agents then worked inside those instructions.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>

### gateways: Agents hunt exposed AI gateways (ai)

Planner, worker, verification, and compression agents share a knowledge graph and record searches for One API, New API, Veloera, Sub2API, LiteLLM, Dify, FastGPT, Langflow, RAGFlow, Open WebUI, LobeChat, and Ollama.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>

### keys: Stolen keys fund more hunting (impact)

The records describe keys being validated, graded, pooled, and supplied to the framework gateway. Amazon cannot verify those outcomes.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>

### kyc: Deepfake KYC at two exchanges (ai)

Separate concurrent sessions record real-time deepfake video and forged ID photos aimed at two cryptocurrency exchanges.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>

### rejected: Liveness checks reject the deepfake (impact)

The vendor's server-side verdict rejected the deepfake on every attempt the agents recorded. The document-photo result was not stated.

Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>

### Connections

- instruct -> gateways
- gateways -> keys
- instruct -> kyc
- kyc -> rejected

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016.001](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.001) Software Tools — Amazon assesses the captured agents were running the ARTEX offensive framework.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>
- [AML.T0055](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0055) Unsecured Credentials — The recorded playbook steals provider keys from exposed gateways, default credentials, client-side JavaScript, source maps, debug routes, and environment files.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>
- [AML.T0012](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0012) Valid Accounts — Stolen keys were validated with provider identity services and fed into the framework gateway as working credentials.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>
- [AML.T0088](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0088) Generate Deepfakes — A second campaign recorded real-time deepfake video and forged ID photos aimed at cryptocurrency-exchange KYC checks. Recorded liveness verdicts rejected the deepfake.
  Evidence: <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-observes-threat-actors-running-cybercrime-campaigns-with-artex-ai-agents/>

## Prevention
Put AI gateways, application builders, and inference endpoints under the same controls as other production systems. Change default credentials, including the LiteLLM default master key, and do not leave an install uninitialized. Remove unauthenticated management and provider-configuration endpoints. Review Langflow for CVE-2025-3248 and RAGFlow for CVE-2026-28797, and do not trust an isCliRequest header as authentication on One API family systems. Alert on URL-fetch features that can reach cloud metadata, and on secrets in client-side JavaScript, source maps, debug routes, and environment files. Watch provider spend. Treat the published IPs as MadPot observations of agent traffic in the stated windows, and corroborate them before blocking.

## Detections
- [Web Server Cloud Metadata SSRF Request](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/cross-platform/credential_access_web_server_cloud_imds_ssrf_request.toml) (elastic) — Matched SSRF and 169.254.169.254 with security-detections-mcp. Web-server URL or query hitting a cloud metadata endpoint. Amazon's captured playbook includes SSRF through URL-fetch features to cloud metadata. Amazon did not confirm each attempt succeeded.
- [Cloud Instance Metadata Credential Path HTTP Request](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/network/credential_access_cloud_imds_credential_http_request.toml) (elastic) — Matched 169.254.169.254 with security-detections-mcp. HTTP GET to a metadata credential path. Same playbook item, seen on packet capture rather than the web-server log.
- [Ollama API Accessed from External Network](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/cross-platform/initial_access_ollama_api_external_access.toml) (elastic) — Matched Ollama with security-detections-mcp. Ollama is one of the exposed AI applications the agents' records say they hunted. The rule flags an unauthenticated Ollama API accepting an external connection. It does not identify ARTEX.
- No rule for the KYC deepfake or the named gateway flaws (guidance) — security-detections-mcp searches for deepfake, KYC, liveness, Langflow, RAGFlow, default credential, master key, and source map returned no Sigma, Splunk, or Elastic rule for this activity. A master-key hit was Windows DPAPI, which is a different control. Hunt the LiteLLM default master key, CVE-2025-3248, CVE-2026-28797, the One API family isCliRequest authentication bypass, and secrets in JavaScript bundles, source maps, debug routes, and environment files. There is no detection rule in the index for a rejected liveness deepfake.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-10-03
- Last updated: 2026-11-16
- 2026-11-13 (Amazon Threat Intelligence): CJ Moses publishes the MadPot capture of ARTEX agent memory for 3 October through 5 November 2026, including the credential-hunting playbook, the rejected KYC deepfakes, and eleven IP addresses.
- 2026-11-16 (OpenAttacks): Entry added from the Amazon blog and The Hacker News restatement. Community fork URLs were checked, and the detection rules above were taken from security-detections-mcp matches whose pages returned HTTP 200.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- [ARTEX](https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX) (https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX) — Community-maintained fork; the original repository is unavailable. MoLeft/ARTEX also returned HTTP 200 on 2026-11-16. The original Autumn-27/ARTEX repository returned HTTP 404.
- [ARTEX](https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX) (https://github.com/MoLeft/ARTEX) — Community-maintained fork; the original repository is unavailable. MoLeft/ARTEX also returned HTTP 200 on 2026-11-16. The original Autumn-27/ARTEX repository returned HTTP 404.
- LLM chat-completion APIs, as presented by MadPot
- One API
- New API
- Veloera
- Sub2API
- LiteLLM
- Dify
- FastGPT
- Langflow
- RAGFlow
- Open WebUI
- LobeChat
- [Ollama](https://github.com/ollama/ollama)

### Other tooling
- unknown

### Offensive frameworks
- [ARTEX](https://openattacks.ai/tools/artex/) (`artex`)

## Source notes
The campaigns, the moderate-confidence attribution, the product list, the CVEs, the isCliRequest bypass, the SSRF and secret locations, the knowledge-graph roles, the two exchanges, the rejected liveness checks, and the eleven IPs are from the Amazon blog. Amazon states that it did not watch the off-decoy activity and cannot verify agent-reported outcomes. The Hacker News article of 16 November 2026 restates that blog. It also discusses Cloudflare and other ARTEX reporting that is not part of this entry. The Hacker News flaw table says Langflow versions before 1.3.0 are affected by CVE-2025-3248; Amazon's blog names the CVE and does not give that version range. https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX and https://github.com/MoLeft/ARTEX returned HTTP 200 on 16 November 2026. https://github.com/Autumn-27/ARTEX returned HTTP 404 and is not linked. Mainland China appears only as an operator exclusion, not as a victim region.
