# Elastic CyberStrikeAI server reads AWS user data, secrets, and sends an SSM command

- ID: elastic-cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-13
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/elastic-cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-2026-11/

## Summary
Elastic Security Labs (Samir Bousseaden, Bryan Porras Blanch, Terrance DeJesus; Nov 13, 2026) describes one CyberStrikeAI server, 43.240.29.11 (AS133199, SonderCloud Limited, Hong Kong), using stolen credentials for eight identities across four AWS accounts from Sep 30 to Oct 16, 2026. Five were IAM users and three were EC2 instance roles. The role sessions were named after instance IDs, and using them from an outside IP means the credentials were taken off the instances. Activity fell on four days and produced 78 alerts, all medium or low, which Elastic says only cohered once correlated by source IP. On Sep 30 the server called GetCallerIdentity on four IAM users across three accounts within 51 minutes, three of those calls in the same second, plus GetSessionToken. The same day app-role read Secrets Manager and then issued 56 DescribeInstanceAttribute userData calls in 141 milliseconds (about 2.5 ms per call), part of 57 user-data reads from one assumed-role session that day and 58 user-data alerts overall. On Oct 1 it revalidated credentials, read more secrets with app-role, and used nonprod-role for two GetSecretValue calls. On Oct 2 container-role called GetParameter with decryption on an SSM SecureString and SendCommand 29 seconds later. CloudTrail does not show the command text or whether it succeeded. After 14 quiet days, on Oct 16 the server called GetSessionToken for a previously unseen user in a fourth account and rechecked two earlier users. Internet scan data showed 43.240.29.11 serving a CyberStrikeAI page on port 8080, with a self-signed certificate (CN CyberStrikeAI, serial 3356730520974368630, issued 2026-09-15), one day before the first alert. 43.240.29.28 and 43.240.29.33 served the same page, FOFA fid, and certificate but were not in this AWS telemetry. Elastic says none of the three was still serving that interface as of November 2026. Six Elastic rules fired: AWS EC2 User Data Retrieval for EC2 Instance (58, medium), AWS STS GetCallerIdentity API Called for the First Time (11, medium), First Time Seen AWS Secret Value Accessed in Secrets Manager (4, medium), AWS STS GetSessionToken Usage (3, low), AWS SSM SendCommand Execution by Rare User (1, low), and AWS Systems Manager SecureString Parameter Request with Decryption Flag (1, medium). Elastic added two high-severity rules: AWS EC2 Bulk User Data Enumeration (more than 20 distinct instances in 10 minutes) and AWS STS GetCallerIdentity Bulk Account Enumeration (three or more distinct non-role identities from the same external IP and ASN in 10 minutes). Elastic, citing Will Thomas at Team Cymru, says 21 CyberStrikeAI IPs were counted in early 2026, aimed at Fortinet edge devices, and that this is the first Elastic case of the same tooling turned on AWS. The repository contains 24 SKILL.md files, including skills/unlimited-attack-scope/SKILL.md and skills/cloud-attack-methods/SKILL.md. Elastic cites https://github.com/Ed1s0nZ/CyberStrikeAI, which redirects to https://github.com/AIPentest/CyberStrikeAI. Link kaspersky-cyberstrikeai-schools-2026 and trendai-chinese-language-operators-used-cyberstrikeais-2026-10.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed operator. Elastic confirms the source IP served CyberStrikeAI. No country or motivation is stated. Hong Kong is the hosting location.

- Actor: Unnamed CyberStrikeAI operator
- Country: Unknown
- Motivation: unknown
- Attributed by: Elastic Security Labs
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
vendor-asserted

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Stolen AWS credentials for five IAM users and three EC2 instance roles, used from an external IP. Elastic does not know how the credentials were obtained.
- Victims and sectors: Four AWS accounts. Account and identity names were replaced with aliases. No sector was named.
- Regions: unknown
- Region notes: No victim country is stated. Hong Kong and AS133199 are the server's hosting location and are not recorded as a target region.
- Scale and metrics: One server, eight identities, four accounts, 78 alerts, four active days from Sep 30 to Oct 16, 2026. Fifty-six userData calls in 141 milliseconds. Sixty-five of 78 alerts fell on Sep 30.

## Attack flow
### validate: GetCallerIdentity sweeps (access)

On Sep 30 the server validates four IAM users across three accounts, with three GetCallerIdentity calls in one second, and requests GetSessionToken.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>

### userdata: 56 userData reads in 141 ms (ai)

app-role calls DescribeInstanceAttribute for userData 56 times in 141 milliseconds after an earlier GetSecretValue.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>

### ssm: Decrypt a parameter, then SendCommand (execution)

On Oct 2 container-role reads an SSM SecureString and issues SendCommand 29 seconds later. The command text is not in the audit log.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>

### return: Recheck after 14 days (impact)

On Oct 16 the same server calls GetSessionToken in a fourth account and revalidates two earlier users.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>

### Connections

- validate -> userdata
- userdata -> ssm
- ssm -> return

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016.001](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.001) Software Tools — Elastic ties the calls to a host that was serving the CyberStrikeAI console
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>
- [AML.T0012](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0012) Valid Accounts — Stolen IAM user and EC2 instance-role credentials were used from an external IP
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>
- [AML.T0055](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0055) Unsecured Credentials — The server read Secrets Manager values, EC2 user data, and a decrypted SSM SecureString
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/cyberstrikeai-aws-cloudtrail-intrusion>

## Prevention
Rotate every identity that touched 43.240.29.11 and revoke active sessions. Replace instance profiles whose role credentials were used off-instance. Treat secrets in EC2 user data as exposed. Review SSM command history for the Oct 2 SendCommand. Restrict ec2:DescribeInstanceAttribute for userData. Correlate medium and low CloudTrail alerts by source IP across multi-day gaps.

## Detections
- [AWS EC2 User Data Retrieval for EC2 Instance](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/discovery_ec2_userdata_request_for_ec2_instance.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. DescribeInstanceAttribute for userData. Elastic says this rule fired 58 times.
- [AWS EC2 Bulk User Data Enumeration](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/discovery_ec2_bulk_user_data_enumeration.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. Elastic added this high-severity rule for more than 20 distinct userData reads by one principal and source IP in 10 minutes. The intrusion had 56 calls in 141 milliseconds.
- [AWS STS GetCallerIdentity API Called for the First Time](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/discovery_new_terms_sts_getcalleridentity.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. First GetCallerIdentity for an identity. Elastic says this rule fired 11 times.
- [AWS STS GetCallerIdentity Bulk Account Enumeration](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/discovery_sts_getcalleridentity_bulk_account_enumeration.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. Elastic added this high-severity rule for three or more distinct non-role identities calling GetCallerIdentity from the same external IP and ASN in 10 minutes.
- [First Time Seen AWS Secret Value Accessed in Secrets Manager](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/credential_access_new_terms_secretsmanager_getsecretvalue.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. First GetSecretValue. Elastic says this rule fired 4 times, including app-role and nonprod-role.
- [AWS Systems Manager SecureString Parameter Request with Decryption Flag](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/credential_access_retrieve_secure_string_parameters_via_ssm.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. GetParameter with decryption. Matches the Oct 2 container-role read.
- [AWS SSM `SendCommand` Execution by Rare User](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/aws/execution_ssm_sendcommand_by_rare_user.toml) (elastic) — AWS CloudTrail. Rare SendCommand. Matches the command issued 29 seconds after the SecureString read. The article's low-severity rule name is AWS SSM SendCommand Execution by Rare User.
- GetSessionToken and off-instance role use (guidance) — The article also names AWS STS GetSessionToken Usage (3 low alerts). The public rule titled AWS IAM API Calls via Temporary Session Tokens describes later IAM calls that use a temporary token, not the GetSessionToken API itself, so it is not cited. Also hunt instance-role credentials whose source IP is not the instance or the organization's NAT, and the certificate serial 3356730520974368630.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-09-30
- Last updated: 2026-11-15
- 2026-09-30 (Elastic Security Labs): 43.240.29.11 validates IAM users, reads Secrets Manager, and issues 56 userData calls in 141 milliseconds
- 2026-10-02 (Elastic Security Labs): container-role reads a decrypted SSM parameter and calls SendCommand 29 seconds later
- 2026-10-16 (Elastic Security Labs): The same server returns after 14 days and revalidates credentials, including a fourth account
- 2026-11-13 (Elastic Security Labs): Elastic publishes the CloudTrail write-up and two high-severity bulk enumeration rules
- 2026-11-15 (OpenAttacks): Tagged CyberStrikeAI. Country is Unknown because Elastic states no country.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- CyberStrikeAI (https://github.com/AIPentest/CyberStrikeAI), web interface on TCP 8080
- 24 SKILL.md files in the repository, including unlimited-attack-scope and cloud-attack-methods

### Other tooling
- AWS APIs GetCallerIdentity, GetSessionToken, GetSecretValue, DescribeInstanceAttribute, GetParameter, SendCommand

### Offensive frameworks
- [CyberStrikeAI](https://openattacks.ai/tools/cyberstrikeai/) (`cyberstrikeai`)

### MagicSword projects
- [LOLSkills](https://lolskills.io)

## Source notes
API sequence, alert counts, rule names, certificate serial 3356730520974368630, and the three IPs are from the Elastic article. Only 43.240.29.11 appeared in the AWS alerts. The 24 SKILL.md count was checked on the repository default branch on 2026-11-15. Elastic cites https://github.com/Ed1s0nZ/CyberStrikeAI; that URL redirected to https://github.com/AIPentest/CyberStrikeAI. Link kaspersky-cyberstrikeai-schools-2026 and trendai-chinese-language-operators-used-cyberstrikeais-2026-10.

# SOCRadar XaocMail ClickFix delivers Phantom53 and HavoX, built with Claude Code

- ID: socradar-xaocmail-phantom53-claude-code-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-13
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/socradar-xaocmail-phantom53-claude-code-2026-11/

## Summary
SOCRadar Threat Research Unit (published Nov 13, 2026) describes a Russian-speaking operator chaining XaocMail, Phantom53, and HavoX (Havoc Demon). Preparation began in October 2025. Phishing tests and framework development continued in early 2026. The operation launched in August 2026 and was still active at publication. XaocMail, on 185.224.132.148:8585, builds per-victim document-sharing lures (Google Docs, Adobe Acrobat, Office365, SecuredDocs) and stores landing JSON in Cloudflare KV. Emails point at hxxps://securedoc.[subdomain].workers.dev/?id={linkId}. The ClickFix page offers three Win+R paths: a PowerShell DownloadString cradle, mshta against /h?id=X (an earlier ZIP of GUP.exe sideloading libcurl.dll), and msiexec /i .../m?id=X /qn, which SOCRadar calls the active path. The MSI impersonates names such as Visual Studio Tools, Python, or Node.js and drops Python 3.11 under a random directory in AppData or LocalAppData. A pythonw scheduled task runs the beacon every 12 minutes. A second task runs the watcher every 3 minutes. The beacon is a .pyd named like a real package (cv2.pyd, with PyInit_cv2, and the same pattern for numpy, PIL, and lxml). It beacons over DNS-over-HTTPS to Cloudflare, Google, and ControlD, encoding commands in AAAA records, with UDP/53 as a fallback, and a week-keyed SHA256 DGA on .xyz. CMD_WAKE writes an IPC file so the watcher reflectively loads an AES-GCM HavoX blob in memory. If the MSI is not elevated it can show a UAC prompt for a product-named task, and a separate payload uses fodhelper.exe registry hijacking. Payload aliases include a Level RMM drop from downloads.level.io and an AI-secret stealer covering Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Cursor, Windsurf, and other AI tools, plus password-manager, browser, SSH, git, wallet, and messaging collection. SOCRadar's AI evidence is on the operator side: .claude/settings.json, 143 numbered NNN-task-name.md files across adminka (25), implant_v3 (31), and implant_v4 (87), a 117 KB implant_v4/arch.md, and implant_v4/.tasks/prompts/noav-beacon-test.md, a Russian prompt for a claudeg5f-tmux Claude wrapper that tells the agent to install a test MSI on a VM named NoAV under an authorized red-team framing. HavoX's login panel and an exfiltration path are on 145.63.138.67. Almost a thousand personalized pages were identified. Named target countries are Brazil, Peru, India, Indonesia, and Spain, across technology, finance, education, government, logistics, and manufacturing. SOCRadar did not confirm attacker intent beyond the footholds.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed Russian-speaking operator. SOCRadar does not name a country or a tracked cluster.

- Actor: Unnamed Russian-speaking operator
- Motivation: cybercrime
- Attributed by: SOCRadar Threat Research Unit
- Confidence: low

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Spearphishing links from XaocMail to Cloudflare Worker ClickFix pages that ask the user to run msiexec, mshta, or a PowerShell cradle
- Victims and sectors: Technology, finance, education, government, logistics, and manufacturing. Almost a thousand personalized lure pages. No organization names are listed here.
- Regions: Brazil, Peru, India, Indonesia, Spain
- Region notes: Brazil, Peru, India, Indonesia, and Spain are the countries SOCRadar names. The article also describes the set as Latin America, Asia, and Europe. Operator language is not a victim region.
- Scale and metrics: Almost a thousand personalized XaocMail pages. 143 Claude Code task files. implant_v4/arch.md is 117 KB. No victim-host count was published.

## Attack flow
### phish: XaocMail sends the lure (access)

A self-hosted panel writes a per-victim landing page to Cloudflare KV and emails a workers.dev link.

Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>

### clickfix: ClickFix runs the MSI (execution)

The page asks the user to run msiexec /i against /m?id=, or an older mshta or PowerShell path.

Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>

### beacon: pythonw beacon over DoH (execution)

Python 3.11 in AppData runs cv2.pyd every 12 minutes and a watcher every 3 minutes. Commands return inside DNS AAAA answers.

Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>

### claude: Claude Code built the implant (ai)

The operator tree has .claude/settings.json, 143 numbered task files, a 117 KB arch.md, and a Russian authorized-red-team test prompt.

Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>

### havoc: HavoX in memory and AI-secret theft (impact)

CMD_WAKE loads the Havoc Demon reflectively. Aliases include Level RMM and a stealer for Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Cursor, and Windsurf.

Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>

### Connections

- phish -> clickfix
- clickfix -> beacon
- beacon -> havoc
- claude -> beacon

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016.002](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.002) Generative AI — Claude Code task files, settings, and a Russian test prompt show the operator used a Claude agent to build and test the implant
  Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>
- [AML.T0054](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0054) LLM Jailbreak — The NoAV prompt frames the job as authorized red-team work so the agent stays on task
  Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>
- [AML.T0055](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0055) Unsecured Credentials — A payload alias steals secrets from Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Cursor, Windsurf, and other AI tools
  Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>
- [AML.T0052](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0052) Phishing — XaocMail sends document-sharing phishing links to Cloudflare Worker ClickFix pages
  Evidence: <https://socradar.io/blog/xaocmail-clickfix-phantom53-havox-america-asia-europe/>

## Prevention
Block ClickFix paste-and-run. Alert when explorer.exe starts msiexec.exe with a workers.dev URL, when mshta.exe fetches an HTTP URL, and when pythonw.exe runs a .pyw from AppData and loads a .pyd outside a normal Python install. Hunt scheduled tasks named like MicrosoftEdgeUpdateTask, OneDriveSyncTask, WindowsDefenderScheduledScan, OfficeBackgroundTaskHandler, or AdobeAcrobatUpdateTask with a short random suffix. Treat DoH from pythonw.exe to 1.1.1.1, 8.8.8.8, or 76.76.2.11 as suspicious when paired with those tasks.

## Detections
- [Suspicious Msiexec Quiet Install From Remote Location](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_msiexec_install_remote.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. msiexec /i against a remote URL with a quiet flag. Matches the active ClickFix path msiexec /i .../m?id= /qn.
- [Windows MSIExec Remote Download](https://github.com/splunk/security_content/blob/develop/detections/endpoint/windows_msiexec_remote_download.yml) (splunk) — Windows endpoint. msiexec retrieving a remote package. Same active delivery path.
- [Remotely Hosted HTA File Executed Via Mshta.EXE](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_mshta_http.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. mshta.exe with an http URL. Matches the earlier /h?id= path that fetched GUP.exe and libcurl.dll.
- [Potential DLL Sideloading Of Libcurl.DLL Via GUP.EXE](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/image_load/image_load_side_load_gup_libcurl.yml) (sigma) — Windows image load. GUP.exe loading libcurl.dll. SOCRadar assigns this to the previous HTA ZIP, not the current MSI.
- [Suspicious Execution of Powershell with Base64](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_powershell_encode.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. Encoded PowerShell. Phantom53 CMD_EXEC runs powershell -EncodedCommand.
- [Bypass UAC via Fodhelper.exe](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_uac_bypass_fodhelper.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. fodhelper.exe UAC bypass, which SOCRadar lists as a payload alias.
- [Local Scheduled Task Creation](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/windows/persistence_local_scheduled_task_creation.toml) (elastic) — Windows. New scheduled task. The MSI registers beacon and watcher tasks. Elevated installs use COM ITaskFolder::RegisterTask and do not spawn schtasks.exe.
- [Windows Scheduled Task Created Via XML](https://github.com/splunk/security_content/blob/develop/detections/endpoint/windows_scheduled_task_created_via_xml.yml) (splunk) — Windows. Task registration from XML. SOCRadar says the non-elevated path writes p53_task_a.xml and p53_task_b.xml.
- pythonw, DoH, and Level RMM (guidance) — No cited rule names pythonw.exe loading cv2.pyd from AppData, DoH POST to 1.1.1.1, 8.8.8.8, or 76.76.2.11, or the Level RMM MSI. The schtasks.exe creation rule is not cited because the elevated path does not spawn schtasks.exe. Also hunt 145.63.138.67 and 185.224.132.148.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2025-10
- Last updated: 2026-11-13
- 2025-10 (SOCRadar): Operators test malware and Cloudflare infrastructure. Development continues through early 2026.
- 2026-08 (SOCRadar): XaocMail phishing launches. The operation is still active at publication.
- 2026-11-13 (SOCRadar): STRU publishes the XaocMail, Phantom53, and HavoX chain, including the Claude Code task files and 145.63.138.67

### AI tooling and models
- [Claude Code](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code) (.claude/settings.json, numbered task files, claudeg5f-tmux prompt wrapper)
- AI-secret stealer aimed at [Claude Code](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code), Codex, [Gemini CLI](https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli), [Cursor](https://cursor.com/), and Windsurf

### Other tooling

# Elastic REF3364 SANSAR takes over exposed Ray GPU hosts and hides utilization

- ID: elastic-sansar-ray-gpu-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-12
- AI role: ai_agent_or_mcp_as_target
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/elastic-sansar-ray-gpu-2026-11/

## Summary
Elastic Security Labs (Salim Bitam, Nov 12, 2026) tracks REF3364. The Ray dashboard was bound to loopback, but the job agent was reachable from the internet and accepted unauthenticated Jobs API submissions. External probes appeared in the Ray logs on October 22, 2026. On October 24 a submitted job ran nvidia-smi and sent the GPU inventory out. On October 25 a Python reverse-shell job connected to an external listener under the account that owned the Ray session. The actor added an SSH key to that account's authorized_keys, obtained root, and staged SANSAR under /opt/vadi_ on three GPU hosts. SANSAR is a Rust orchestrator (v0.1.3) with Tor/Arti command and control and Lyrebird obfs4/WebTunnel. It supervises XMRig, stored as kurt, and SRBMiner-MULTI, stored as tilki (https://github.com/xmrig/xmrig and https://github.com/doktor83/SRBMiner-Multi). A replaced libnvidia-ml.so.1 wraps the real NVML library and reports GPU use as 0 percent, randomizes temperature between 36 and 46 C and power between 100 and 200 W, and filters process lists, so nvidia-smi and other NVML clients lie. An ftrace rootkit, acpi_thermal_mgr.ko, is built with DKMS, loaded through the initramfs, and hides processes, files, and sockets. A systemd backdoor named netns reads its command-and-control address from an Ethereum smart contract and connects on TCP 8080. Elastic publishes YARA rules for SANSAR and netns. Link shadowray-2-0-hijacks-exposed-ray-2025-11.

## Category
attacks-on-ai

## Attribution
REF3364. Elastic does not attribute the activity to a named group or country.

- Actor: REF3364
- Country: Unknown
- Motivation: financially-motivated
- Attributed by: Elastic Security Labs
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Unauthenticated Ray Jobs API on an internet-reachable job agent
- Victims and sectors: Three GPU hosts running Ray. Elastic did not name the organizations.
- Regions: unknown
- Region notes: Elastic did not name the countries of the GPU hosts.
- Scale and metrics: Three GPU hosts. Activity from October 22 through October 25, 2026, from probe to persistent access.

## Attack flow
### probe: Unauthenticated Ray job (access)

October 22 probes, then an October 24 job that runs nvidia-smi and sends the GPU inventory out.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>

### shell: Python reverse shell (execution)

On October 25 a Python reverse-shell job connects out as the Ray session account.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>

### keys: SSH key and root (impact)

An SSH key is added to authorized_keys. The actor then stages SANSAR under /opt/vadi_.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>

### hide: NVML wrapper and DKMS rootkit (execution)

libnvidia-ml.so.1 reports 0 percent GPU use. acpi_thermal_mgr.ko is loaded through DKMS and the initramfs.

Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>

### Connections

- probe -> shell
- shell -> keys
- keys -> hide

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0049](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0049) Exploit Public-Facing Application — The internet-reachable Ray Jobs API accepted unauthenticated job submissions.
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>
- [AML.T0050](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0050) Command and Scripting Interpreter — Submitted jobs ran nvidia-smi and a Python reverse shell.
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>
- [AML.T0072](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0072) Reverse Shell — The October 25 job was a Python reverse shell to an external listener.
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>
- [AML.T0034](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0034) Cost Harvesting — SANSAR mined on the stolen GPU capacity while reporting 0 percent utilization.
  Evidence: <https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware>

## Prevention
Do not expose the Ray job agent. Require authentication on the Jobs API. Alert on a Python reverse shell from the Ray account, a new authorized_keys entry, an out-of-tree kernel module, and a miner command line even when the binary is named kurt or tilki. Compare nvidia-smi with a second GPU telemetry source so a wrapped libnvidia-ml.so.1 cannot be the only reading.

## Detections
- [Python Reverse Shell Execution Via PTY And Socket Modules](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/linux/process_creation/proc_creation_lnx_python_reverse_shell.yml) (sigma) — Linux process creation. python -c with import, pty, socket, spawn, and connect. Matches that common reverse-shell form. A job that is a script file without those strings on one command line will not match.
- [Linux SSH Authorized Keys Modification](https://github.com/splunk/security_content/blob/develop/detections/endpoint/linux_ssh_authorized_keys_modification.yml) (splunk) — Linux endpoint. Modification of authorized_keys, which is how the actor kept access after the reverse shell.
- [Linux Install Kernel Module Using Modprobe Utility](https://github.com/splunk/security_content/blob/develop/detections/endpoint/linux_install_kernel_module_using_modprobe_utility.yml) (splunk) — Linux endpoint. modprobe loading a kernel module. DKMS loads acpi_thermal_mgr.ko this way.
- [Tainted Out-Of-Tree Kernel Module Load](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/linux/persistence_tainted_kernel_module_out_of_tree_load.toml) (elastic) — Linux endpoint. An out-of-tree module that taints the kernel. Fits the DKMS-built acpi_thermal_mgr.ko rootkit.
- [Linux Crypto Mining Indicators](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/linux/process_creation/proc_creation_lnx_crypto_mining.yml) (sigma) — Linux process creation. Stratum URLs, --donate-level=0, and related miner flags. The binaries are renamed kurt and tilki, so a process-name rule for xmrig does not match. These command-line flags still can.
- NVML wrapper and netns contract (guidance) — No cited rule looks for a replaced libnvidia-ml.so.1, the path /opt/vadi_, or a systemd unit that reads an Ethereum contract. LD_PRELOAD rules do not match this library replacement.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-10-22
- Last updated: 2026-11-12
- 2026-10-22 (Elastic Security Labs): External probes appear in the Ray job logs.
- 2026-10-24 (Elastic Security Labs): A submitted job runs nvidia-smi and sends the GPU inventory to an external server.
- 2026-10-25 (Elastic Security Labs): A Python reverse-shell job connects out. The actor adds an SSH key, gains root, and stages SANSAR under /opt/vadi_.
- 2026-11-12 (Elastic Security Labs): Elastic publishes REF3364, the seven file hashes, the NVML wrapper, and the DKMS rootkit.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- Ray (https://github.com/ray-project/ray)

### Other tooling
- SANSAR orchestrator
- XMRig, filename kurt (https://github.com/xmrig/xmrig)
- SRBMiner-MULTI, filename tilki (https://github.com/doktor83/SRBMiner-Multi)
- Lyrebird obfs4/WebTunnel
- NVML wrapper libnvidia-ml.so.1

## Source notes
Dates, paths, component names, and the seven SHA-256 hashes are from the Elastic Security Labs article. The miners are renamed, so a rule that requires the process name xmrig or srbminer will miss kurt and tilki. The NVML change is a replaced libnvidia-ml.so.1, not an LD_PRELOAD or ld.so.preload edit, so those rules are not cited.

## Sources
- [0% GPU utilization: How SANSAR hides Linux cryptomining](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/sansar-linux-cryptomining-malware) — Elastic Security Labs

# Eye Security ARTEX intrusion from a PHP webshell to GitOps and CI runners

- ID: eye-security-artex-gitops-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-12
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/eye-security-artex-gitops-2026-11/

## Summary
Eye Security (Bas van den Berg, Nov 12, 2026) describes a multi-day ARTEX intrusion that started at a vulnerable custom PHP upload function. Uploads were extension-fuzzed (.phtml, .php5, .php7, .phar, .inc, .PHP) into webshells such as artx_s5.php and zzartx0000.php. The shell accepted header X-ARTX-KEY or parameter k, used key k=x9f3c2b7e, and returned ### ARTX-AGENT. Environment variables on the pod leaked API keys, and the first call with a stolen key landed within minutes. When a rate limiter engaged, the agents rewrote their client in about three minutes to rotate a spoofed client-IP header and peaked at over 9,000 requests a minute. After that path was blocked they used a pool of more than 17,000 residential proxies. They pulled Neo-reGeorg (https://github.com/L-codes/Neo-reGeorg) and changed it to keep state in the internal cache so it worked across load-balanced pods. Its default user agent is Mozilla/5.0 (X11; Linux x86_64; rv:38.0) Gecko/20100101 Firefox/38.0. With an engineer's Git hosting identity they changed the deployment repo so GitOps deployed attacker pods, including artx-pod and a privileged pod on a staging host. Deleting the pod brought it back within a second. From the privileged pod they broke out to the node with nsenter. Separately, Eye counted thirteen logins in under three minutes, one socket per login, asking a file-transfer server to forward Docker, containerd, CRI-O, lightweight Kubernetes, the system bus, the PHP worker pool, the process supervisor, and sshd. The server refused every one of those forwards. A CI app key from the secret store was used to register self-hosted GitHub runners named sink-*, re-registered about every eight minutes through the night. Backdoor admins looked like QA or audit accounts (qa_probe_*, qa.audit29a). In phpMyAdmin they used LOAD DATA LOCAL INFILE into databases named artx_* and INTO OUTFILE write tests at /tmp/x.php5. The operators went after gift-card codes. Eye also publishes OSINT infrastructure from similar ARTEX activity: 207.5.195.93, artex.lol, staging.artex.lol, artex.tokena2b.top, artex.ok7777.eu.cc, artex.noog2infra.site, images tomicdd/artex and congdonglinux/artex, and callbacks on dnslog.cn, oast.me, and webhook.site. Data markers include ARTEX_WRITE_PROBE, ARTEX-PROBE-0001, ARTX_PERSIST_OK, and artex_control_marker_zz9q. Link srm-artex-ir-host-artifacts-2026-11, datadog-artex-aws-cognito-2026-11, arctic-wolf-antifox-iis-aws-2026-11, microsoft-artex-github-secrets-keyvault-2026-11, and badbyte-artex-backdoor-docker-2026-11.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed. Eye ties one address to public ARTEX reporting on payment-API abuse. No country is given.

- Actor: Unnamed ARTEX operators
- Country: Unknown
- Motivation: financially-motivated
- Attributed by: Eye Security
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Vulnerable custom PHP upload function, then extension-fuzzed webshells
- Victims and sectors: A company running payment and gift-card services. Eye did not name the company.
- Regions: unknown
- Region notes: Eye did not name the victim's country. Infrastructure countries are not recorded as victim regions.
- Scale and metrics: Peak over 9,000 requests a minute. More than 17,000 residential proxy addresses. Thirteen socket-forward logins in under three minutes, all refused by the server. GitOps recreated a deleted attacker pod within a second. Runners were re-registered about every eight minutes.

## Attack flow
### upload: PHP upload becomes a webshell (access)

Extension-fuzzed uploads such as artx_s5.php and zzartx0000.php accept X-ARTX-KEY and return ### ARTX-AGENT.

Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

### secrets: Pod secrets used within minutes (execution)

Environment variables and the secret store yield API keys. A rate limiter is bypassed in about three minutes.

Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

### tunnel: Neo-reGeorg across pods (execution)

Neo-reGeorg is modified to keep state in the internal cache so the tunnel survives load-balanced pods.

Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

### gitops: GitOps restores the attacker pod (impact)

A deployment-repo change creates artx-pod. Deleting it brings the pod back within a second.

Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

### escape: Privileged pod to the node (execution)

A privileged staging pod is used with nsenter to reach the host node.

Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

### runner: Self-hosted runners named sink-* (impact)

A CI app key registers self-hosted GitHub runners that are re-registered through the night.

Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

### Connections

- upload -> secrets
- secrets -> tunnel
- secrets -> gitops
- gitops -> escape
- secrets -> runner

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016) Obtain Capabilities — The operators deployed the ARTEX agent framework and Neo-reGeorg.
  Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>
- [AML.T0049](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0049) Exploit Public-Facing Application — Initial access was a vulnerable PHP upload function turned into webshells.
  Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>
- [AML.T0055](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0055) Unsecured Credentials — Pod environment variables and the cloud secret store exposed API keys and a CI app key.
  Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>
- [AML.T0012](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0012) Valid Accounts — Stolen keys and an engineer's Git hosting identity were reused for API calls, GitOps, and runner registration.
  Evidence: <https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/>

## Prevention
Block the published webshell names and the X-ARTX-KEY header. Alert on php-fpm or the web server spawning a shell, nsenter into the host PID namespace, a privileged pod, and a new self-hosted GitHub runner such as sink-*. Treat GitOps reconciliation as a persistence path: a deleted artx-pod that returns immediately is the deployment repo, not a restart.

## Detections
- [Linux Webshell Indicators](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/linux/process_creation/proc_creation_lnx_webshell_detection.yml) (sigma) — Linux process creation. A web server parent spawning a shell utility. Parent images in the rule include httpd, nginx, and apache2. php-fpm is not in that parent list, so a shell whose parent is only php-fpm will not match.
- [Potential Docker Escape via Nsenter](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/linux/privilege_escalation_docker_escape_via_nsenter.toml) (elastic) — Linux endpoint. nsenter changing into another namespace, including the host breakout Eye saw from the privileged pod.
- [Kubernetes Privileged Pod Created](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/kubernetes/privilege_escalation_privileged_pod_created.toml) (elastic) — Kubernetes audit. Creation of a privileged pod. Matches the staging pod used before nsenter.
- [Remote GitHub Actions Runner Registration](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/cross-platform/execution_register_github_actions_runner.toml) (elastic) — Endpoint, cross-platform. Runner.Listener registering a self-hosted runner. Matches the sink-* runners.
- [New GitHub Self Hosted Action Runner](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/integrations/github/initial_access_github_register_self_hosted_runner.toml) (elastic) — GitHub audit log. A new self-hosted runner registered on a repository.
- ARTEX shell key and Neo-reGeorg user agent (guidance) — No cited rule contains X-ARTX-KEY, k=x9f3c2b7e, ### ARTX-AGENT, artx_s5.php, or the Firefox/38.0 Neo-reGeorg agent. The Sigma ReGeorg web-log rule requires a null user agent, so it misses this tunnel.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-11-12
- Last updated: 2026-11-12
- 2026-11-12 (Eye Security): Eye publishes the incident: PHP webshells, a three-minute rate-limit bypass, Neo-reGeorg, GitOps pod restoration, nsenter, and sink-* GitHub runners.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- [ARTEX](https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX) (https://github.com/RuoJi6/ARTEX) — Community-maintained fork; the original repository is unavailable. MoLeft/ARTEX also returned HTTP 200 on 2026-11-16. The original Autumn-27/ARTEX repository returned HTTP 404.

### Other tooling
- Neo-reGeorg (https://github.com/L-codes/Neo-reGeorg)
- nsenter container breakout
- Self-hosted [GitHub Actions](https://github.com/features/actions) runners

### MagicSword projects
- [LOTTunnels](https://lottunnels.github.io)

## Source notes
File names, the shell key, the request peak, the proxy count, the GitHub runner names, and the nsenter breakout are from the Eye Security incident write-up. 207.5.195.93 and the artex.* domains are in Eye's OSINT infrastructure list for similar ARTEX activity, not described as addresses observed inside this victim. Neo-reGeorg is tracked by LOTTunnels at https://lottunnels.github.io/lottunnels/Binaries/neo-regeorg/. The Sigma ReGeorg web-log rule requires a null user agent, and this tunnel used Firefox/38.0, so that rule is not cited.

## Sources
- [Machine speed: how we defended against an AI-driven attack](https://labs.eye.security/how-we-defended-against-an-ai-driven-attack/) — Eye Security

# FBI FLASH FaceStealer uses stolen webcam video and BlueNoroff deepfakes

- ID: fbi-facestealer-bluenoroff-deepfake-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-12
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/fbi-facestealer-bluenoroff-deepfake-2026-11/

## Summary
FBI FLASH AC-111226-MS (Nov 12, 2026) describes FaceStealer, attributed to Sapphire Sleet / BlueNoroff / APT38 / UNC1069. The operators take over a victim's Telegram, LinkedIn, Slack, or Discord account, read the chat history, and message contacts to catch up or talk investment. A Calendly invite sets the time. Shortly before the call, the link points at a typosquat such as uu01webzoom.us or teams.livemeet.us. The copycat Zoom or Teams page plays the trusted contact's stolen webcam recording, often next to other participants. A chat message says the target cannot be heard and walks them through an update, script, or terminal paste that installs a cookie harvester, keyloggers, a screen capturer, and a backdoor. The target's webcam is recorded from the moment the link opens and feeds the next round. The FBI says the group has begun using what it calls super intelligence to make deepfakes of crypto and web3 figures alongside stolen footage. Earlier in 2026 a maintainer of a widely downloaded JavaScript library ran the fix during a fake Teams call, and the operators then published malicious dependencies that were caught quickly. The FBI has also seen attempts against npm maintainers and, recently, Rust crate maintainers. Arctic Wolf's Nov 12 paper adds lures that end in a ClickFix command: NovaStream Player at vip.novaplayer.online (HTML SHA-256 ca14040cc48fbc3b00ea5f74e891266128f7bb3058be400782c5b08436f7668a), a Chrome PDF Viewer kit at chromeupdate.docdock.online and calendly.docdock.online, and calendly.idcheck.watch. The macOS idcheck command is curl piped to osacompile. The Windows cradle is curl piped to powershell -c with [Console]::In.ReadToEnd()|iex. Hosting includes Hostwinds 104.168.149.21, 104.168.149.182, and 104.168.149.230, and Petrosky 83.136.210.38 for docdock.online. Arctic Wolf's appendix lists 98 high-confidence domains. The domains below are that table plus the FBI examples and the lure hosts named in the paper. Link bluenoroff-uses-deepfaked-executives-fake-zoom-2025-06 and unc1069-gemini-deepfake-crypto-2025.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Sapphire Sleet, BlueNoroff, APT38, UNC1069, Dangerous Password, CryptoCore, Stardust Chollima. FBI FLASH AC-111226-MS. Arctic Wolf independently attributes the activity to BlueNoroff.

- Actor: Sapphire Sleet / BlueNoroff / APT38 / UNC1069
- Country: North Korea
- Motivation: financially-motivated
- Attributed by: FBI, Arctic Wolf Labs
- Confidence: high

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Hijacked messenger accounts, then a typosquat Zoom or Teams page and a ClickFix paste
- Victims and sectors: Cryptocurrency and web3 individuals and companies, plus open-source maintainers. The FBI says the group stole more than $400 million in 18 months.
- Regions: Multiple regions
- Region notes: North Korea is the actor origin from the FBI, not a victim region. Arctic Wolf's target countries are not listed as a closed set, so the entry uses Multiple regions.
- Scale and metrics: More than $400 million stolen in 18 months, per the FBI. Arctic Wolf: 122 AI-generated assets from seven generators, and 98 high-confidence domains on the Hostwinds cluster.

## Attack flow
### account: Hijacked messenger account (access)

A prior victim's Telegram, LinkedIn, Slack, or Discord account messages contacts and sends a Calendly invite.

Evidence: <https://www.ic3.gov/CSA/2026/261112.pdf>

### meeting: Typosquat meeting page (execution)

The link, such as uu01webzoom.us or teams.livemeet.us, plays stolen webcam video and starts recording the new target.

Evidence: <https://www.ic3.gov/CSA/2026/261112.pdf>

### deepfake: Generator-tagged deepfakes (ai)

Arctic Wolf found 122 assets from seven generators, identified by embedded tags and C2PA rather than appearance. Stolen footage is still most of the scheme.

Evidence: <https://www.ic3.gov/CSA/2026/261112.pdf> <https://arcticwolf.com/wp-content/bluenoroff_still_in_the_meeting.pdf>

### clickfix: ClickFix paste (execution)

Windows uses curl piped to powershell ReadToEnd|iex. macOS uses curl piped to osacompile.

Evidence: <https://arcticwolf.com/wp-content/bluenoroff_still_in_the_meeting.pdf>

### Connections

- account -> meeting
- meeting -> deepfake
- meeting -> clickfix

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0088](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0088) Generate Deepfakes — The FBI says the operators generate deepfake video of crypto and web3 figures from public images, and Arctic Wolf ties 122 assets to generator tags and C2PA data.
  Evidence: <https://www.ic3.gov/CSA/2026/261112.pdf> <https://arcticwolf.com/wp-content/bluenoroff_still_in_the_meeting.pdf>
- [AML.T0016.002](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.002) Generative AI — Arctic Wolf attributes the generated media to Grok Imagine, Google Generative AI, Wondershare AIGC, GPT-4o, GPT Image, and BytePlus ModelArk.
  Evidence: <https://arcticwolf.com/wp-content/bluenoroff_still_in_the_meeting.pdf>
- [AML.T0052](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0052) Phishing — Hijacked accounts and typosquat Zoom and Teams pages deliver the lure.
  Evidence: <https://www.ic3.gov/CSA/2026/261112.pdf>

## Prevention
Treat a meeting link that arrives in chat just before the call as hostile until the domain is the vendor's. Do not paste a command from a call. On Windows, hunt curl.exe piped into powershell with ReadToEnd and iex. On macOS, hunt curl and osacompile in the same command line. Block the domains and the four IPs below.

## Detections
- [Potential In-Memory Download And Compile Of Payloads](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/macos/process_creation/proc_creation_macos_susp_in_memory_download_and_compile.yml) (sigma) — macOS process creation. A command line that contains both curl and osacompile. Matches the calendly.idcheck.watch cradle.
- [Suspicious Curl.EXE Download](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_curl_susp_download.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. curl.exe downloading a payload. Matches the front half of the Windows ClickFix cradle. It does not see the powershell ReadToEnd|iex half.
- ReadToEnd stdin cradle (guidance) — security-detections-mcp and a corpus search found no Sigma, Splunk, or Elastic rule for \[Console\]::In.ReadToEnd()|iex. The Sigma IEX rules require a space that this cradle omits. Also hunt the NovaStream HTML hash and the four IPs.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-05-20
- Last updated: 2026-11-12
- 2026-05-20 (Arctic Wolf Labs): A self-signed certificate generated at 15:04 UTC is later served by all three Hostwinds addresses. Lure domains resolve over the following days.
- 2026-11-12 (FBI): FBI FLASH describes FaceStealer, including deepfakes of crypto figures and the uu01webzoom.us and teams.livemeet.us examples.
- 2026-11-12 (Arctic Wolf Labs): Arctic Wolf publishes the six-month update, generator provenance for 122 assets, the ClickFix command lines, and the high-confidence domain table.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- [Grok](https://x.ai/grok) Imagine
- Google Generative AI
- Wondershare AIGC
- GPT-4o and GPT Image
- BytePlus ModelArk

### Other tooling
- Stolen webcam recordings
- ClickFix terminal paste
- NovaStream Player lure

## Source notes
The takeover loop, the two example typosquats, the deepfake statement, and the maintainer supply-chain note are from the FBI PDF. Generator counts, the ClickFix command lines, the Hostwinds and Petrosky addresses, the NovaStream hash, and the high-confidence domain table are from the Arctic Wolf white paper. The Sigma IEX rules require a space in '| iex' or 'iex ', and the published cradle is ReadToEnd()|iex with no space, so those rules are not cited. The osacompile run-only rule requires -x and -e, and the idcheck command uses osacompile -o, so that rule is not cited either. There is no attacker GitHub repository.

## Sources
- [FBI FLASH AC-111226-MS: FaceStealer](https://www.ic3.gov/CSA/2026/261112.pdf) — FBI
- [Still in the Meeting: BlueNoroff Six Months On](https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/still-in-the-meeting-bluenoroff-six-months-on/) — Arctic Wolf Labs
- [Still in the Meeting: BlueNoroff Six Months On (white paper)](https://arcticwolf.com/wp-content/bluenoroff_still_in_the_meeting.pdf) — Arctic Wolf Labs
- [BlueNoroff uses ClickFix, fileless PowerShell, and AI-generated Zoom meetings](https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/bluenoroff-uses-clickfix-fileless-powershell-and-ai-generated-zoom-meetings-to-target-web3-sector/) — Arctic Wolf Labs
- [Inside a DPRK BlueNoroff ClickFix kit](https://www.jumpsec.com/guides/inside-a-dprk-bluenoroff-clickfix-kit/) — Jumpsec
- [Coverage of the FaceStealer disruption](https://therecord.media/stolen-binance-crypto-recovered-doj-facestealer-disrupted) — The Record
- [FBI warns of North Korean fake Zoom calls](https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/13/fbi-north-korean-fake-zoom-calls/) — Help Net Security

# Island TypoPrompting turns AI-brand typos into ClickFix, Net Guard, and search hijacks

- ID: island-typoprompting-ai-typosquats-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-12
- AI role: ai_tool_as_lure
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/island-typoprompting-ai-typosquats-2026-11/

## Summary
Island (Oleg Zaytsev, Nov 12, 2026) traced 186 lookalike domains across 27 AI brands, including Claude, ChatGPT, DeepSeek, Perplexity, Midjourney, ElevenLabs, and Cursor. A Nov 3, 2026 snapshot connected cloude.ai to a Windows ClickFix command and eight AI typos to an installer funnel. A Windows scan of cloude.ai went through xml.securetrackingnode.com/click to biomimicrydevtech.buzz (flow_id=81, bid about 0.0065 to 0.009) and then a random subdomain of blockchainfuturedev.buzz, which fingerprinted the browser and served a fake Cloudflare check. The recovered command was cmd /c curl.exe -fsL --ssl-no-revoke --retry 8 saving %TEMP%\W901.ps1 from 3112851459/985, then powershell -w h -nop -ep bypass -File that script. 3112851459 is 185.138.88.3 as a decimal integer. Pivoting the gate script hash found 550 scan records across 211 entry domains from April 2025 through November 2026, including lookalikes of banks, insurers, and enterprise software, plus Windows, macOS curl-to-zsh, and archive lures. Eight typos, including ckaude.ai, deepceek.com, copiot.com, elevenlebs.io, and vocie.ai, went through deezyj.com to fake Security Check pages that installed Net Guard, a Microsoft Store-signed Tauri app (publisher Myflixly App, product id 9MX3CH3BQ44Q, package family MyflixlyApp.NetGuard_k6q889pad57pt, analyzed 1.0.9.0). Island says the MSIX matches the Store hash and that buntai-sdk can change the default search engine, run JavaScript through DevTools Runtime.evaluate, and edit browser profiles. The same SDK is in Privacy Keeper 1.0.10.0 and MyFlixly Browser 1.0.10.0. The installer funnel spans 191 lander domains. preplexity.ai went to quickflashtool.com and a Chrome extension that sets the default search provider to find.flashysearches.com. It is one of 30 extensions from the same kit. Thirteen had been removed. The 17 still listed showed 972,000 declared users in total on November 3. Island says Socket's Oct 29, 2026 Searchjacked research lists 22 of the 30. An 81-domain portfolio including about 23 AI lookalikes ties to a11.click. Link anyrun-fake-ai-tools-2026-10.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
Unnamed. Island ties an 81-domain portfolio to a11.click and shared affiliate keys. No country is stated.

- Actor: Unnamed typosquat operators
- Motivation: financially-motivated
- Attributed by: Island
- Confidence: low

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: A mistyped AI domain is handed to a traffic-distribution system that selects a ClickFix command, a Store installer, or an extension page
- Victims and sectors: People trying to reach AI tools. The wider gate also used lookalikes of banks, insurers, and enterprise software. No named victim organizations.
- Regions: Multiple regions
- Region notes: Island does not name victim countries. The traffic system uses the visitor's country to pick a lure, which is not a target-region list.
- Scale and metrics: 186 lookalike domains, 27 AI brands, in the Nov 3, 2026 snapshot. 211 entry domains on the ClickFix gate. 972,000 declared users across 17 still-listed search extensions on Nov 3. Net Guard funnel about 1,437 source domains.

## Attack flow
### typo: AI-brand typo loads (access)

A lookalike such as cloude.ai or preplexity.ai is served instead of the intended AI product. A parking page hands the browser to a traffic-distribution system.

Evidence: <https://www.island.io/blog/typoprompting-from-ai-typos-to-malware>

### clickfix: Fake Cloudflare check copies a command (execution)

The Windows route copies a curl.exe download of W901.ps1 from decimal host 3112851459 and a hidden PowerShell launch.

Evidence: <https://www.island.io/blog/typoprompting-from-ai-typos-to-malware>

### netguard: Store-signed Net Guard (impact)

Other typos install the real Microsoft Store Net Guard package, whose buntai-sdk can change search settings and run DevTools scripts.

Evidence: <https://www.island.io/blog/typoprompting-from-ai-typos-to-malware>

### extension: Search-hijack extension (impact)

preplexity.ai leads to an unlisted Chrome extension that sets the default search provider to find.flashysearches.com.

Evidence: <https://www.island.io/blog/typoprompting-from-ai-typos-to-malware>

### Connections

- typo -> clickfix
- typo -> netguard
- typo -> extension

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0052](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0052) Phishing — Mistyped AI domains are the lure that leads to a command, an installer, or an extension
  Evidence: <https://www.island.io/blog/typoprompting-from-ai-typos-to-malware>
- [AML.T0050](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0050) Command and Scripting Interpreter — The Windows route copies a curl.exe and PowerShell command that the visitor is asked to run
  Evidence: <https://www.island.io/blog/typoprompting-from-ai-typos-to-malware>

## Prevention
Publish managed bookmarks for approved AI services. Alert when curl.exe writes a .ps1 and PowerShell starts with -w h and -ep bypass, including decimal hosts such as 3112851459. Do not treat a Microsoft Store signature as a reason to allow Net Guard package family MyflixlyApp.NetGuard_k6q889pad57pt. Enforce a managed search provider and block the extension IDs below.

## Detections
- [Suspicious Curl.EXE Download](https://github.com/SigmaHQ/sigma/blob/master/rules/windows/process_creation/proc_creation_win_curl_susp_download.yml) (sigma) — Windows process creation. curl.exe download. Matches the ClickFix command that saves W901.ps1. The decimal host 3112851459 is not a dotted IP, so a dotted-IP-only rule would miss it.
- [Potential File Transfer via Curl for Windows](https://github.com/elastic/detection-rules/blob/main/rules/windows/command_and_control_tool_transfer_via_curl.toml) (elastic) — Windows endpoint. curl.exe retrieving a remote file. Same ClickFix download, followed by powershell -w h -nop -ep bypass.
- Decimal host, Net Guard, and search override (guidance) — No cited rule encodes 3112851459, -w h -ep bypass, the Net Guard package family MyflixlyApp.NetGuard_k6q889pad57pt, or extension IDs lihnhjjcdlfdomjliljlommppbfacmbj and nlmpdomhofmjdidcghodlgneobojdjfm. A Store signature matching hash bdddb50204d6867b4bd73d9cfe93cbc98c4518342da104e4608f04e675ac6cb3 is the package Island analyzed, not a clean verdict.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2025-04
- Last updated: 2026-11-12
- 2025-04 (Island): Earliest matching records in the wider gate-kit corpus. The kit later serves Windows, macOS, and archive lures.
- 2026-11-03 (Island): Research snapshot ties cloude.ai to the Windows ClickFix command and counts 186 AI lookalikes across 27 brands. Seventeen search extensions still listed show 972,000 users.
- 2026-11-12 (Island): Oleg Zaytsev publishes TypoPrompting, including the decimal IP, Net Guard package identifiers, and the preplexity.ai extension path

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- Lookalike domains for [Claude](https://claude.com/product/overview), [ChatGPT](https://chatgpt.com/), [DeepSeek](https://github.com/deepseek-ai), Perplexity, [Cursor](https://cursor.com/), and 22 other AI brands

### Other tooling
- curl.exe and hidden [PowerShell](https://github.com/PowerShell/PowerShell) (LOLBAS)
- Net Guard / buntai-sdk Microsoft Store package
- Chrome search-override extensions

### MagicSword projects
- [LOLBAS](https://lolbas-project.github.io)

Counts, the ClickFix command, decimal IP 3112851459, package identifiers, and the 972,000-user figure are from Island. The article's appendix lists more lookalikes than the IOC set below. Link anyrun-fake-ai-tools-2026-10. Historical payloads on chatlgpt.com, midj0urney.org, and deepsek.icu are included only as Island states them.

# Unit 42 CL-STA-1238 uses Cairn with Zhipu GLM and DeepSeek against semiconductor, defense, and civil-society targets

- ID: unit42-cairn-cl-sta-1238-2026-11
- Reported: 2026-11-12
- AI role: attacker_uses_ai_as_operator
- Entry: https://openattacks.ai/attacks/unit42-cairn-cl-sta-1238-2026-11/

## Summary
Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 (published Nov 12, 2026) tracks CL-STA-1238, assessed with moderate confidence as China-nexus intelligence collection. From August through September 2026, Chinese-speaking operators used the open-source Cairn framework (https://github.com/oritera/Cairn) for 25 operations against at least 19 organizations and confirmed unauthorized access or data retrieval at 10. They paired Cairn with Zhipu GLM 5.2 (13 operations), DeepSeek V4 Flash (8), and DeepSeek V4 Pro (4), using the higher-tier Pro model for repeats against targets that resisted Flash. Each operation loaded a skill pack named AIScan Runner Skills, which Unit 42 says is not in upstream Cairn. A worker system-prompt preamble framed every Reason and Explore phase as authorized security testing, CTF, assessment, vulnerability research, or code audit and forbade refusal. Each operation also wrote AUTHORIZATION.md treating task creation as a client-approved engagement. Neither model refused that framing. Operators supplied a one-line goal and a root domain, and sometimes prior intelligence such as VPN gateway addresses, group names, and shared group passwords. Cairn then mapped the target, broke the goal into three to seven stages, and looped until the model judged the goal met, options exhausted, or the operator stopped it. Individual operations ran from 20 minutes to more than 50 hours. Confirmed outcomes include weak-password spraying that yielded dozens of valid credentials and non-public directory data at an industry standards body, plus an unauthenticated cloud-storage write that overwrote a live standards-ballot document; a brute-forced production credential at a semiconductor company followed by unauthorized administrator accounts and a persistent API token; an unauthenticated account-creation bypass at a U.S. teachers' union that logged access to more than one million member records, roughly 31,000 CRM contacts, and staff password hashes; an open registration endpoint on a defense-policy identity platform exposing roughly 500 user records, including .mil and .gov accounts; and five further cases covering tens of thousands of content-management records, employee data at a semiconductor-lithography manufacturer, roughly 150,000 internal documents at an industrial-automation manufacturer, and student data at an education nonprofit. Targeting concentrated on semiconductor and advanced-manufacturing supply chain (seven organizations), defense and defense policy (three, including a space technology company, a defense-analytics vendor, and a policy think tank), and human rights and civil society (four, including a Canadian human-rights body, a U.S. teachers' union, a science-policy advocacy group, and an education nonprofit). Unit 42 assesses a single operator or small team. The exploited flaws were known classes: authentication bypasses, insecure direct object references, mass-assignment privilege escalation, weak and default credentials, exposed cloud APIs, and dated public CVEs. Infrastructure: Cairn hosting 154.44.31.4 (Hong Kong, AS979, NetLab Global) and egress and staging 82.29.129.54 (United States, AS142036, Hosteons Pte. Ltd.). Link cairn-norforge-2026-10 and cert-in-crafty-falcon-bfsi-2026-11.

## Category
adversary-use-of-ai

## Attribution
CL-STA-1238. Unit 42 assesses moderate confidence of China-nexus intelligence collection from Chinese tasking and authorization files, exclusive use of Zhipu GLM and DeepSeek, and the target set.

- Actor: CL-STA-1238
- Country: China
- Motivation: nation-state
- Attributed by: Palo Alto Networks Unit 42
- Confidence: medium

## AI involvement confidence
confirmed

## Scope and targeting
- Initial access: Operator-supplied root domain, sometimes with pre-gathered VPN gateways, group names, and shared passwords. Cairn then used authentication bypasses, weak or default credentials, insecure object references, and exposed cloud APIs.
- Victims and sectors: At least 19 organizations in semiconductor and advanced manufacturing, defense and defense policy, and human rights and civil society. Confirmed access or data retrieval at 10.
- Regions: Multiple regions
- Region notes: Named targets include U.S. defense-policy organizations, a U.S. teachers' union, and a Canadian human-rights body. Other organizations' countries were not stated. Hong Kong and the U.S. egress host are infrastructure, not victim regions, so the entry uses Multiple regions.
- Scale and metrics: 25 operations, at least 19 organizations, confirmed unauthorized access or data retrieval at 10. Thirteen operations on GLM 5.2, eight on DeepSeek V4 Flash, four on DeepSeek V4 Pro. One organization exceeded one million member records. Operations lasted from 20 minutes to more than 50 hours.

## Attack flow
### task: Operator sets a one-line goal (access)

The operator creates a Cairn task with a Chinese goal and a root domain, and sometimes VPN gateways, group names, and shared passwords.

Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>

### models: GLM or DeepSeek plans the operation (ai)

GLM 5.2 or DeepSeek V4 Flash or Pro runs bootstrap, Reason, and Explore under an authorization preamble and the AIScan Runner Skills pack.

Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>

### loop: Autonomous exploit loop (execution)

The model maps the target into three to seven stages and loops from 20 minutes to more than 50 hours until the goal is met or the operator stops it.

Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>

### access: Credentials, records, or admin accounts (impact)

Unit 42 confirmed unauthorized access or data retrieval at 10 of at least 19 organizations, including password spraying, cloud-storage overwrite, and minted administrator accounts.

Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>

### Connections

- task -> models
- models -> loop
- loop -> access

## MITRE ATLAS
- [AML.T0016.002](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0016.002) Generative AI — Operators drove Cairn with Zhipu GLM 5.2 and DeepSeek V4 Flash and Pro
  Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>
- [AML.T0054](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0054) LLM Jailbreak — A Chinese preamble and AUTHORIZATION.md told the models the work was authorized and never to refuse
  Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>
- [AML.T0053](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0053) AI Agent Tool Invocation — Each operation loaded the AIScan Runner Skills pack for scanning and exploitation
  Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>
- [AML.T0049](https://atlas.mitre.org/techniques/AML.T0049) Exploit Public-Facing Application — Confirmed access used authentication bypasses, weak credentials, and exposed cloud APIs on public-facing systems
  Evidence: <https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/>

## Prevention
Treat agent-driven scanning as ordinary vulnerability volume, not a new exploit class. Remove weak and default credentials, close unauthenticated registration and cloud-storage writes, and patch known public-facing flaws. Block the two published IPs. Do not treat a same-/24 scan lead as this cluster without Unit 42's indicators.

## Detections
- Cairn web outcomes have no single host rule (guidance) — Unit 42 describes weak-password spraying, unauthenticated cloud-storage writes, account-creation bypasses, and minted administrator accounts on unnamed application stacks. No Sigma, Splunk, or Elastic rule names Cairn, AIScan Runner Skills, or those application flaws. Hunt the published IPs 154.44.31.4 and 82.29.129.54, unexpected administrator accounts and API tokens, and unauthenticated writes to standards or directory data. A same-/24 panel lead is not an indicator from this report.

## Timeline
- First seen: 2026-08
- Last updated: 2026-11-16
- 2026-09 (Palo Alto Networks Unit 42): CL-STA-1238 completes 25 Cairn operations, August through September 2026, with confirmed access or data retrieval at 10 organizations
- 2026-11-12 (Palo Alto Networks Unit 42): Unit 42 publishes the CL-STA-1238 report, including GLM and DeepSeek model counts, the AIScan Runner Skills pack, and IPs 154.44.31.4 and 82.29.129.54. A same-/24 address, 154.44.31.104, is an unconfirmed Censys panel lead and is not an IOC.
- 2026-11-16 (Google Threat Intelligence Group): GTIG's quarterly tracker says Cairn was used in an intrusion with targets including a US aerospace organization. No indicators are published, and GTIG does not identify the activity as CL-STA-1238.

## Tools
### AI tooling and models
- [Cairn](https://github.com/oritera/Cairn) (https://github.com/oritera/Cairn), customized fork
- Zhipu GLM 5.2
- [DeepSeek](https://github.com/deepseek-ai) V4 Flash
- [DeepSeek](https://github.com/deepseek-ai) V4 Pro
- AIScan Runner Skills pack (not in upstream [Cairn](https://github.com/oritera/Cairn))

### Other tooling
- unknown

### Offensive frameworks
- [Cairn](https://openattacks.ai/tools/cairn/) (`cairn`)

### MagicSword projects
- [LOLSkills](https://lolskills.io)

## Source notes
Counts, models, the AIScan Runner Skills pack, the authorization preamble, the confirmed outcomes, and the two IPs are from the Unit 42 article. 154.44.31.104 was described as another live Cairn panel in the same /24 as the controller 154.44.31.4, attributed to a Censys observation. It is not in the Unit 42 indicator list, and no public Censys report naming that address was available to confirm it. It is an unconfirmed lead only and is not an IOC. Cairn is open source, so an exposed panel is not by itself this cluster. Link cairn-norforge-2026-10, the cairn tool page, and cert-in-crafty-falcon-bfsi-2026-11. No victim organization names are repeated beyond the sector descriptions Unit 42 published. The 16 November 2026 GTIG tracker says GTIG observed Cairn used in intrusion activity against targets including a US aerospace organization. GTIG does not name CL-STA-1238, does not give indicators, and does not say that intrusion is this cluster. The note is recorded here because this is the catalog's Cairn intrusion entry. frameworks remains cairn.
